At Mr. Atherton's suggestion I asked Mr. Gromyko to
call this afternoon and informed him or the pertinent
parts of the telegram dated September 5, 1942 from
Chungking relative to the Chinese interpretation of the
10
resignation of Togo, the Japanese Foreign Minister.
In contrast to this interpretation I also informed
Mr. Gromyko of the interpretation given to this event
by reports from neutral souroes in Europe (Vichy's tele-
gram po. 1303, September 5, 1 p.m.) to the effeot that
the resignation was due primarily to the reactions of
Mr. Togo to the creation of the Greater East Asia
Ministry in the Japanese Government.
PS/DLB
Mr. Gromyko thanked me for giving him this informa-
tion which he stated was of definite interest to the
Soviet Embassy.

=== Page 2 === (confidence 0.99)
September 8, 1942.
In a telegram from Chungking dated September 5, 1942,
the American Ambassador to China reports, inter alis, that
the ohief of one of the departments in the Chinese Foreign
Orfice who has had experience in Japan interprets the
assumption by General Tojo of the portfolio of Foreign
Minister in Japan as an indication that the diplomatio
phase of Japanese-Soviet Union relations has reached the
point of failure and that General Tojo is prepared, unless
a final demand for an arrangement satisfactory to Japan
is met, to take military aotion.
In contrast to this opinion, there has come to the
Department of State a report giving estimate of a highly
plaoed offioial in the Far East of a neutral power. There
is attributed to the said offioial a statement of opinion
to the effeot that the displacement of Foreign Minister Togo
probably was due to opposition by Togo to General Tojo's
plan for the oreation of a speoial agenoy to deal with
Japan's relations with other areas in the "Greater East
Asia Prosperity Sphere".

Captain MoCrea, Naval Aide to the President, called
me on the telephone this noon and stated that the
Secretary of the Navy had mentioned in press conference
894.00/
this morning a telegram which MoCrea thought was from
Gauss at Chungking giving the Chinese estimate of the
implications of the displacement of Foreign Minister Toge
from the Japanese Cabinet and assumption by General Tojo
9
of the Foreign Offioe portfolio. The Chinese estimate
6
was, according to Captain MoCrea's account of the telegram,
that this meant that the Japanese military leadership,
having made certain demands upon the Soviet Government,
11
intended, unless they received in the near future from
S
the Soviet Government a favorable reply, to attack the
LLB
Soviet Union.
The President had asked Captain MoCrea to bring the
above to the attention of Mr. Welles and to suggest that
Mr. Welles bring it to the attention of the Soviet Ambas-
sador. Captain MoCrea had disoovered that Mr. Welles is
not in town and he therefore was informing me.
I stated that I would call the matter to the attention

Copy No. 
OFFICE OF STRATEGIC SERVICES
Research and analysis Branch
Far Eastern Section.
INTERVIEW No. 16
(Supplement to Report No. 77: Information Gathered on
the S. S. Gripsholm)
With: Mr. Russell L. Durgin
Date: August 1942
Subject: Conditions in Tokyo
Place: S. S. Gripsholm
after Dec. 7, 1941
INTRODUCTION
Mr. Durgin, who spent some 20 years with the YMCA in Tokyo, had
an unusually wide acquaintance among Japanese. Since after the out-
break of war in the Facific Mr. Durgin was one of only a few
Americans who were free to move about in Tokyo, the information which
he was able to give about conditions in that city during the first
half of this year is of unusual value.
TREATMENT OF AMERICANS
From the point of view of the treatment by the Japanese
after December 7, 194l, Mr. Durgin classed americans in Japan as
follows: (1) Free, (2) interned at home, (3) interned in camp, and
(4) imprisoned. Of the 130 Protestant missionaries in Japan about
110 came into the hands of the Japaneso authorities because of their
citizenship. about 60 were interned; the others were free -- that is
free within the city of residence. In the group which was imprisoned
(6152)

=== Page 7 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
-2 -
were two american women -- Alice Grube of Osaka and Phyllis argyl
of Tokyo -- although women more often than men were allowed to move
about freely. In Tokyo, for instance, only seven merican men, of
whom Mr. Durgin was one, were "free"; the others were A. J. Stirewalt,
R. D. McCoy, M. S. Frehn, Dr. Henry Topping, J. F. Gressitt, and
Paul S. Mayer.
Mr. Durgin believes that his acquaintance with police offi-
cials, due to the service he had rendered in connection with two trials,
may have accounted for his being "free." When police called and
searched Mr. Durgin's house, as they did those (f others, Mr. Durgin
asked about his status and was told that, wiile he could not luave
Tokyo, he could use his discretion in going about the city. On sub-
sequent visits, the police expressed an interest in whether he got
food enough.
Mr. Durgin's wife started for the United States on Ducember 2
on the "Tatsuta Mamu," which turned back and arrived in Yokohama on
December 15. Mrs. Durgin was held in a hotel in Yokohama until the
twenty-first, when she was allowed to leave after securing a guarantee
for her hotel bill. During the week she was in Yokohama she had no
change of clothes. She was unable to see any rhyme or reason in the
treatment of herself or many of the others.
SHORTAGES
During the six months following December 7, Mr. Durgin was
able to make personal observations of conditions in Tokyo. Since he
had no servants and did his own marketing he was in a position to
know the prices and shortages of food particularly. General figures

=== Page 8 === (confidence 0.98)
-3 -
CONPIDENTTL
on food supplies after war started he could not supply. In the
circumstances in which he found himself he feli it advisable not to
be too inquisitive. The rationing of food was controlled through
tickets and by local groups, his own local group being made up of
nine households. These groups are called tonari gumi and ranged from
5 or 6 to 20 households. The final determination as to the allotting
of tickets seems to have been made in a general office for the city.
Distribution of the tickets was through the head of a group to the
houses in his group.
Control of the distribution of rice begun in May or June
1941. Some revision in the amounts allotted was made at about the
time thut the Pacific war began, but up until June 1942 there had
been no further change. There wus complaint under the surface about
the scarcity of rice, and it was the general belief that in families
with growing boys there was not sufficient food. another cause of
complaint was the mixing of the rice, especially when it was mixed
with inferior gruins. Rice tickets could be turned in for bread.
Other commodities which c.une under the ticket system were
sugar (like rice, controlled before December 7, 1941), shoyu, miso,
sult, fish, meit (one-quarter pound once a month) and clothing (which,
after Februury 1942, was put on the point system).
The fish deuler with whom the Durgin's daalt hud 305 fumilies
as regular customers. When fish was available a sign was put up in
front of his shop -- "Nos. 1-75," say, -- and families with those
numbers sent representatives who lined up to buy fish. Next day per-
haps the sign would aay "Nos: 76-200." The dealer estimated how much

=== Page 9 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
CONFIDENTIAL
he had to divide and took care of as many as he could from day to
day. (Mr. Durgin heard no mention of the danger of submarines in
connection with the shortage of fish.)
Commoditiés not on the ticket system but obtainable only
through the tonari gumi were: Eggs (after March, four. or five a.
month for two persons) and chicken (a slice once a month).
In general, the people just didn't eat eggs, meat, or fish --
fish perhaps about once a week. While Japanese and foreigners were
treated alike, to some extent the Durgins had an advantage in that
they got a little help from friends.
Foreign groceries such as spaghetti and macaroni were very
scarce, and jams and preserves not buyable. The difficulty of getting
vegetables was explained by Mr. Durgin as due to transportation prob-
lems, to lack of manpower, and to price control. There were always
long lines of women waiting to buy vegetables, which were handled in
the same way as fish.
After a time it became very difficult to get milk. From the
end of april the Durgin's were unable to get milk from the milkman who
had been serving them. He introduced Mr. Durgin to a man from another
ward who was likewise unable to provide milk. Mr. Durgin went to the
police for a milk ticket. He got none. He went to the ward officials.
Still no ticket. It was possible to apply for milk for invalids, but
Mr. Durgin preferred not to make such an application. He went to
the general office for Tokyo where the chief of the economics section
proved to be an old student of his. Through him Mr. Durgin finally
succeeded in getting milk.

=== Page 10 === (confidence 0.99)
-5 -
Clothing was put on a rationing system in February 1942.
One hundred points were allowed per person per year, 60 of which
might be used during the first 6 months. Six pairs of tabi were
allowed per year. Enough cloth for a kimona took 25 points, and a
rain coat, 30 points. Silk things called for fewer points than those
of rayon or cotton mixture.
The shortage of leather was very evident and shoes were
very expensive. Shoes formerly made for ¥20 cost ¥40 or ¥50 in May
or June 1942, if they could be bought at all, and were obviously of
inferior leather. athletic equipment was under very strict control.
Electricity, light, and gas were limited to some fraction
of the consumption of the previous year. Cas was so strictly limited
that, according to Mr. Durgin, it got so that one could not take a
bath.
Bank accounts were also controlled. However, while Mr. Durgin
spoke of "freezing" as involving americans, and of general controls
over the accounts of Japanese, he was not well informed as to the
nature of these general controls. There was discussion in the papera
of the reorganization of the Bank of Japan but, again, Mr. Durgin
knew none of the details except that the reorganization had to do with
the Coprosperity Sphere.
Hundreds of automobiles were idle or jacked up. This,
Mr. Durgin explained by the lack of rubber or gasoline, probably gaso-
line. There was no kerosene or gasoline purchasable after about
Ootober 1940. Before that time three gallons of gasoline a month
had been obtainable. as to rubber, in March or april of this year
rubber bulls were distributed to the school onildren as evidence of

=== Page 11 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
CONFIDENTIAL
the arrival of "treasure-ships" from the south.
The men in charge at various hospitals said that there was
a shortage of medicine and complained particularly of the lack of
ether.
Nails (during the past year), sheet iron, and, in general,
commoditias made of metal were not purchasable. There was a great
effort to bring in by voluntary contributicns scrap metal of all sorts.
at a meeting in Tokyo where voluntary contributions of scrap metal
were asked for, the Japanese secretary of the nerican School ugreed
to give up the metal fence around the school. For this he got f2o0,
although it cost f1,600 to take it down and put up a picket fence in
its place. When the secretary usked why in view of all the things
coming from the south, it was necessary to give up everything of metal,
there was no answer, he told lir. Durgin, excupt a smile. at this
meeting there was grumbling and vigorous complaint. In fact, one man
expressed himself so strongly that the secretary of the American School
was astounded.
POLITICAL CONDITIONS
The interview turned to political matters. Mr. Durgin stated
in reply to a question that there has been general pressure toward
unity in Japan, which he believed was a result to be expected of war.
The differences betwean the gendarmerie and army, about which others
spoke, was not ap arent to Mr. Durgin.

=== Page 12 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
JAPAN'S RELATIONS WITH GERMANY AND ITALY
Up to about 6 months ago Germany exerted considerable
Anfluence in Japan, but the Germane are not happy about things there
today. For the past 6 months Germans and Italians have bean more
olosely watched than have the Americans; for instance, when a Misei
party was being held at Mr. Durgin's at the same time a dance was
taking place at a German residence in the neighborhood, thare was no
interest on the part of the police in the Nisei party, but the Germans
were called in, questioned closely, and asked about every person at
the party.
Mr. Durgin was able to learn little about Jupunese trade
with'Germany or Italy during the war. He said that the Italian com-
murcial attaché told him in the spring thut some trade was going
through, but gave no definite information as to details, except that
Fiat cars had gotten through to Japan since December 194l. This
Mr. Durgin did not verify. There was no information at all about
trade with Germany.
JAPANESE MORALE
Price control was regarded by Mr. Durgin as the chief cause
of dispute and dissatisfaction among the people. The control of prices
is regarded by the Japanese furmer in particular as unfair. at the
same time there was no evidence of other olashes of interest in con-
neotion with price control. While the people felt that they were
being checked and surrounded on all sides, they accepted this situa-
tion because they recognised the fundamental importance of the plans
of the military.

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.98)
-8 -
CONFIDENTIAL
ATTITUDE TOWARD THE WAR
The attitude of the people whom Mr. Durgin knew toward the
: present war is hard to estimate. It was difficult to get people whom
he knew to talk ahout the war. The accepted opinion emphasizes the
attack upon British and american imperialism in the Far Est. Japan
is constantly said to be "freeing".others. In this connection, how-
ever, Mr. Durgin mentioned Tojo's speech of last January which prom-
ised freedom to the Philippines and was followed by many statements
of plans for the Philippines in the future, plans quite inconsistent
with any real freedom from Japanese control.
n Navy spokesman, Hiraidi, frequently during the spring
warned Japan it should be prepared for 200 years of war. In the
opinion of one of Mr. Durgin's civilian friends there may be some
basis for this statement. The usual statement in Japan, however, refers
to a 10-year war.
Mr. Durgin spoke of a conversation he had with Matsuoka about
the middle of 1940, during which Matsuoka made the following remark:
"My heart is with merica, but my head is with Japan." During this
same conversation Matsuoka came near to predicting the trip he made
to urope at a later time. This trip Mr. Durgin pointed out came to
an end just before the German attack on Russia.
APaN AND THE LEAGUE OF NATIONS
at the end of our interview, Mr. Durgin referred to an
ditorial which he had read in a Japanese periodical in about the.
pear 19l9. The editoril..asserted that the creation of a League was
* highly desirable thing; it would require, however, certain supre-
national features. The only power in the world, said the editorial,

=== Page 14 === (confidence 0.95)
- 9 -
OONFIDENTIAL
with a viewpoint above that of the nation and of national interests
is the Emperor of Japan. The conclusion was that the only hope of
success for a League lay in making the Emperor of Japan the head of it.

although it cost f1, 600 to take it down and put up a picket fence in
its place. When the secretary usked why in view of all the things
coming from the south, it was necessary to give up everything of metal,
there was no answer, he told ir. Durgin, excupt a smile. at this
meeting there was grumbling und vigorous complaint. In fact, one man
expressed himself so strongly that the secretary of the American School
was astounded.
POLITICAL CONDITIONS
The interview turned to political matters. Mr. Durgin stated
in reply to a question that there has been general pressure toward
unity in Japan, which he believed was a result to be expected of war.
The differences betwean the gendarmerie und army, about which others
spoke, was not ap arent to Mr. Durgin.

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7-
JAPAN'S RELATIONS WITH GERMANY AND ITALY
Up to about 6 months ago Germany exerted considerable
influence in Japan, but the Germans are not happy about things there
today. For the past 6 months Germans and Italians have bean more
closely watched than have the Americans; for instance, when a Nisei
party was being held at Mr. Durgin's at the same time a dance was
taking place at a German residence in the neighborhood, there was no
interest on the part of the police in the Nisei party, but the Germans
were called in, questioned closely, and asked about every person at
the party.
Mr. Durgin was able to learn little about Jipunese trade
with Germany or Italy during the war. He said that the Italian com-
mercial attaché told him in the spring thut some trade wus going
through, but gave no definite information as to details, except that
Fiat cars had gotten through to Japan since December 194l. This
Mr. Durgin did not verify. There was no information at all about
trade with Germany.
JAPANESE MORALE
Price control was regarded by Mr. Durgin as the chief cause
of dispute and dissatisfaction among the people. The control of prices
is regarded by the Japanese furmer in particular as unfair. at the
same time there was no evidence of other clashes of interest in con-
nection with price control. hile the people felt that they were
being checked and surrounded on all sides, tlhey accepted this situa-
tion because they recognized the fundamental importance of the plans
of the military.

=== Page 24 === (confidence 0.98)
-8-
CONFIDENTIAL
ATTITUDE TOWARD THE WAR
The attitude of the people whom Mr. Durgin knew toward the
present war is hard to estimate. It was difficult to get people whom
he knew to talk about the war. The accepted opinion emphasizes the
attack upon British and nmerican imperialism in the Far East. Japan
is constantly said to be "freeing" others. In this connection, how-
ever, Mr. Durgin mentioned Tojo's speech of last January which prom-
ised freedom to the Philippines and was followed by many statements
of plans for the Philippines in the future, plans quite inconsistent
with any real freedom from Japanese control.
a Navy spokesman, Hiraidi, frequently during the spring
warned Japan it should be prepared for 200 years of war. In the
opinion of one of Mr. Durgin's civilian friends there may be some
basis for this statement. The usual statement in Japan, however, refers
to a 10-year war.
Mr. Durgin spoke of a conversation he had with Matsuoka about
the middle of 1940, during which Matsuoka made the following remark:
"My heart is with america, but my head is with Japan." During this
same conversation Matsuoka came near to predicting the trip he made
to urope at a later time. This trip Mr. Durgin pointed out came to
an end just before the German atiack on Russia.
JAPAN AND THE LEAGUE OF NATIONS
at the end of our interview, Mr. Durgin referred to an
editorial which he had read in a Japanese periodical in about the.
year 1919. The editoril..asserted that the creation of a League was
a highly desirable thing; it would require, however, certain supra-
national features. The only power in the world, said the editorial,

=== Page 25 === (confidence 0.95)
- 9 -
OONFIDENTIAL
with a viewpoint above that of the nution and of national interests
is the Emperor of Japan. The conclusion was that the only hope of
success for a League lay in making the mperor of Japan the head of it.

KEMORANDUM
JAPANESE OUTLOOK
Social Aspeat
Omitting the nobility and the ruling classes, the social situation of
the popular masses is disastrous. The work of productiom falle almost en-
tirely on the women. It is calculated that there are 10 million women oc-
cupied in agriculture, 2 million in the textile industries, 1,200,000 im
various industries and 1 million in business. It is calculated that there
are 2 million in domestic service. The salaries are in the neighborhoed of
$1.50 Argentine per diem (1 Yen 20 Sen). There is no labor legislatiem or
trade organizations, and Article 17 of the "Tchan Ke Atshu" (Police Code)
considers strikes a crime, and strikers as common malefactors. The people
submit to this situation, partly on account of their Buddhist fatalim and
partly because they are accustomed to denial and sacrifice.
Pelitical Aspect
Political struggles in Japan are almost non-existent. Politics are
led by the nobility and the military classes. Among the military classes
there are several castes which, at the same time as they struggle with the
mobility. fight among themselves. There are three political parties in Japan;
all censidered democratic: the "Kokuminto" party, the"Seiyukai" and the
"Kemsekai", but none of the three has an established program and their
figaring as an opposing factor is only occasional. The struggle between the
military is reduced to two principal castes: the "Shogun" and the "Semurai".
The "Shogum", theoretically fallen frem the political arema in 1870, in
reality exists, and is the most serious force opposing the Emperor, although
composed of courtiers. The other party, the "Samurai", whose motte is
leyalty and obedience to the mperor, eren at the expense of their lives,
is the most mamerous and the most fanatic. It is that which took Japan to
war with Chime, (they are Smintes), and later with the U.S. Its members
ceme from the middle classes (bourgeoisie), whe are completely at variance
with the "Marakin". The "Marakin" are the "new-rich" in Japan, those who
made momey in a short time, especially after the war of l914.
Maral Aapeet
In the morale of the Japanese people, one finds a level mach lower than
that of the most backward oriental country. The "Geishaya", the "Jeraja"
and the restaurants with the "Oiran" and the "Hetairas" form an incredible
mumber. The "Geishaya". place of the Geishas, are dens of corruptien,
the Geishas being merely "educated" professienals, educated frem yeuth in
their prefessien. These places are preferred by the "Barakin" on asseant ef
their high prices. There are some 20,000 Geishas in Japan.
The "Jereja" (pepular brothels) possess, 1n Japam, 200,000 inmates. ("Jere")

=== Page 31 === (confidence 0.93)
- 2 -
The Restaurants which have the "oiran" and the Hetairas" (Girls) who
exeroise freely the profession of prostitution, mumber 60,000 adheramts.
All this 1s legalised by the State and tolerated by "Seciety" as a pablie
good.
Intritual Aspect
In this aspect, the Japanese people is an intricate malgamatien.
Shimtos, Beddhists, Confucionists, Evangelists, Catholics are all represented,
in the order given. But always, the basis of unity is the family, of which
the "Semier Family", that which composes the grandparents and their ramifi-
eatiems, is the heed, and over this latter there is the "Kem" (clan), a gremp
of various families, joimed by geographical relationship, or belemging to
a commen place.
Sancluciene
Leeerding to the Japanese synthesis of history with regard to the rising
of Japam as a Matien: "After various generations of celestial divimities, the
grandeem of Amaterasu, Nonigi No. Mi Kote occupied Hinga, where there follewed
three of his descendants, the third, Jimme Teme being the one who founded the
Eapire, handing over the government to the Haman Gods, whose descendant, after
25 centaries, today governs Japan"
The Japamese Constitution is founded on the besic principle that the
Emperer is the first and greatest divinity, and states: "The Japanese Empire
will be gevermed by a dynasty of Emperors, contimmous and uminterrapted,
antil Btermity."
Lamch
The Japamese residente in western countries (taking the Argentime as a
besis) accastemed te eivilised life, are able to mcogaise the backwardmess
of Japes, although through selidarity or necessity or through conferming to
the Baddkist faith, de met publish the fact. Their quietade is due more to
their comformist faith than te amy other camse. In spite of the "Wipem Sawa".
in spite of the "Black Dragon" sect, and in spite of the offieial and semi-
effieial emissaries, visible and invisible, of Japan, there are in existemee
in the Argeatime, partisans of the three political parties mentiemed abeve.
there are moralists, leaders, and, abeve all, Japaaese whe, meoustemed to
westerm culture, are ashamed of the social orgamisatien of their couatry.
There are the "Narakim" whe kmow that at home they are hamiliated, aad there
are the variess religiens divisiens whieh, if ome day they beeame aware of
the existemee of a Ged whe was not their sovereign, wuld make the pelitieal
beses of Japan shake. All those elements, altheugh matagenistie among them-
selves, eeuld meve tewards a single end. Sarreptieieasly at first, ees-
tiensly later eheesing the persens with eare, aad werking under ocover, they
conld ferm a eurrent smtagenistie to the geverament af the Japemese Eapire

Elements of Weakness in the Japanese People
and in the Japanese Position
light of Japan's military suocesses since
194l, upsetting many estimates which had been
her military resources, equipment and prowess and
the qualities of her people, one might be inclined to think
that the Japanese are perhaps invincible. And indeed they
are very formidable, possessing social, personal and
military virtues and an increasingly strong strategic
position that makes them a dangerous and deadly foe.
However, even Achilles had his vulnerable heel, and Japan
has many weak spots.
In the matter of material capacity for modern warfare,
1t is unwise to belittle Japanese resources and potentiali-
ties, especially in view of vast stores of goods and
FEB
FILED
raw material resources that have fallen into Japanese
4.00
8
hands since the beginning of the war. Nor is it wise to
1943
ascribe to the Japanese inferior craftsmanship in the
manufacture of modern weapons of war or inferior skill in
handling such weapons. The evidence is to the contrary.
Neverthelels, without going into detail and without making
comparisons in respect to craftsmanship or skill in handling
weapons, it is a fact that Japan is-still in process of
becoming industrialized whereas we are the leading in-
dustrial nation of the world and that when we get into our
planned

=== Page 35 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
planned stride of naval construction and of plane,
tank or ordnance production, such construotion and
produotion will far exceed Japan's. This being
the case, it is simple arithmetio that, if this
superior weight of armament on sea, land and air
1s brought to bear long enough upon Japan, com-
pelling her to employ incessantly all her weapons,
there will come a point when her war output will
lag behind consumption.
We should not look for political weaknesses
within Japan in wartime.
In 1ssues with for-
eigners, Japanese invariably band together against
the alien, regardless of the justice of the case,
and it may be expected that in the national peril
1nvolved in a war with America the Japanese people will
work as one man to win the war, as they did in the
Russo-Japanese War, when for instance even certain
oollege

=== Page 36 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
college professors too old to fight were said to have dis-
guised themselves as Korean porters and wharf coolies to
py on the enemy. As a corollary of their unfailing devotion
and loyalty to each other, the Japanese in their relations
with non-Japanese have been correspondingly invariably dis-
criminatory and unjust toward non-Japanese races of the
Empire and toward inhabitants of Japanese-occupied territories,
which have utterly alienated these races and inhabitants
from the Japanese. The Germans with their technical leader-
ship and established superiority in certain cultural fields
undoubtedly have some admirers and ready followers among
their neighbors in central Europe, but because of Japanese
cultural inferiority and unjust, greedy and discriminatory
policies toward subject races and neighbors, the Koreans
or Chinese who would not do teir share to hasten Japan's
downfall when the tide begins to turn against the Japa-
nese would be comparatively few.
The unity of the Japanese people in the face of national
danger and in dealing with alien races does not necessarily
imply that there is solidarity or even harmony in the Japanese
social structure. There are deep-seated conflicts between
the urban communities and the farm population, between the
conservative Imperial Court and the extremists, between parliamem-
tarians and politicians on the one side and the bureaucracies
and the

=== Page 37 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
and the fighting services on the other, between mercan-
tile interests and the army, which advocates planned and
state-controlled economy, between those who want Japan to
march with the West and those who want her to return to
the pristine Japanese way of life, between clans and cliques
and classes entrenched in a privileged position and elements
covetous of that position. The more spectacular of the
successive political crises and explosions in Japan dur-
ing the 'thirties and since reflect the inherent disharmony
in Japanese society.
In 1930 Premier Hamaguchi secured ratification of the
London Naval Treaty over the objections of some high naval
officers, but shortly after was attacked and died of wounds
inflicted by a nationalist who disagreed with his policy.
Two years later, when the elderly Tsuyoshi Inukai was
Premier and during a period of acute distress in rural
districts, a bomb was thrown at the carriage of the Emperor
and Empress in a cortege in Tokyo in protest against the
Government's policy of favoring business and financial.
interests at the expense of the farming population. Shortly
after an ex-minister of Finance and the chairman of the
great

=== Page 38 === (confidence 0.99)
-4a-
great meroantile house of Mitsui, both noted for their
liboralism and international cooperation, were slain by
members of the reactionary Blood Brotherhood League. Then
on May 15, 1932, a junta of Army and Navy cadets and junior
officers, impatient with Inukai's measures to reform
parliamentarism and to relieve the farmers, murdered the
old Premier himself. On the same day a group of insurgent
farmers made an abortive effort to cut off Tokyo's
electrical power supply as a demonstration against the
indifference to the suffering in rural districts of the
urban population. After a period of quiet, the struggle
between representative government, international cooperation,
big business and laissez faire economy on the one hand
and reaction, ultra-nationalism and socialistic regulation
on the other flared up in the notorious February 26, 1936,
"Incident", when some 1500 extremist officers and soldiers
in Tokyo mutinied and attempted to assassinate the members
of the cabinet and the leading personages close to the
Emperor, succeeding in the attempt in murdering a number of
Japan's great statesmen. The manifesto of the muniteers
branded all senior statesmen, military cliques, plutocrats,
bureauorats and political parties as traitors.
Harmony

=== Page 39 === (confidence 0.99)
-4b-
Harmony does not even exist within the army itself.
In July l935, for example, Premier Hayashi, himself a
a
General, dismissed General Mazaki,/fire-eating nationalist
notorious for his contempt of representative government and
for his advocacy of state-controlled economy, as Inspector
General of Military Education. In the following month
a Colonel Saburo Aizawa, nationalist fanatic and noted
swordsman, entered Hayashi's office and not finding him
there cut down his assistant, Major General Nagata, instead.
During his trial Aizawa defended his deed on the ground
that it was aimed against elder statesmen, powerful financiers
and bureaucrats and against "the fad of mahjong and caf6s"
(viz., Westernism) which was "corrupting the youth of the
country".
The undercurrent of conflict and the intolerance of
differing opinion in Japanese society are also revealed in the
fact that in the years 1933-36, 59,013* persons were arrested
in Japan on the charge of harboring, preaching or carrying
out "dangerous thoughts", viz., ideas questioning the state
of affairs.
After the February 26, 1936, ,"Incident", with the
progressive cowing of the forces of reason and conservation,
political violence became less common. However, evidences of
the
"Presumably official figures published in the Japan
Times, Foreign Office organ, of August 15, 1936.

=== Page 40 === (confidence 0.99)
-4c-
the gulf between and of the uncompromising spirit of
the several elements of Japanese society were to be found in
the kaleidoscopic changes of government -- there have been
eight cabinets since the February 26, 1936, ,"Indident" --
and the frequent resighations of individual ministers. In
this connection may be mentioned the resignation en masse of
officers of the Foreign Office on October 3, 1939 because
these officials disapproved the establishment of a new
Ministry of Trade that would take over some of their
department's functions. Again in the second half of 194l,
when American-Japanese relations were approaching a crisis,
violence was once more threatened and even flared up to
remove opposition. The ultra-nationalist Tohokai party,
led by Seigo Nakano, issued threats of wholesale assassina-
tion of appeasers in Japanese foreign policy, and Nakano
defied the Home Minister, Baron Hiranuma, to arrest him.
Nakano remained at liberty, but Baron Hiranuma, outstanding
opponent of the Axis alliance and of nationalized economy,
was seriously wounded in two attempts made on his life
within three days in mid-August l94l.
Thus

=== Page 41 === (confidence 0.99)
-4d-
Thus the events that have brought Japan to her
present pass have not represented the composite wishes
of Japanese society, but rather the inflexible will of
headstrong seotions and groups, and if there have not
been in Japan concentration camps for non-conformists
as in Germany, there have been arrests, assassinations
and mutiny to remove and intimidate opposition. It is
obvious that a house as divided within itself as is
Japan will be under great strain in the storm that united
and outraged demooracy will raise for her.
The

=== Page 42 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
The Japanese tactical prinoiple in any sort of physical
competition, especially in war and battle, is to get a light-
ning start and stay in the lead. This principle is based on
the theory, deriving from their own psychology, that the
adversary loses heart if he is behind. In athletio contests
this tendenoy to be overcome by despair and quit when not in
the lead is marked among Japanese. As the present war is not
an athletio contest, with a time limit insufficient to allow
the adversary to even the score or get the lead, but a contest
of infinite time in which to recapture and widen the lead, it
is not at all improbable that this psyohological weakness of
the Japanese may assert itself in unpredictable ways.
The Japanese soldier thinks that he possesses in unsur-
passed degree a virtue known as "gambari". "Gambari" trans-
lated means "stubbornness plus", and a well-known Japanese
proverb says that if one holds on long enough the other
fellow will give in. The Japanese soldier's experience in
his wars since 1895 has on the whole served to give him a
low idea of the other fellow's "gambari". However, in recent
instances where the other fellow has displayed "gambari" the
Japanese soldier has been nonplussed and beaten. At Nomonhan
in l939, for example, when the Russians held their ground and
fought as savagely as the Japanese, the Japanese were glad to
call off the fight after losing some 18,000 men.
Laok

=== Page 43 === (confidence 0.98)
-6-
Laok of inventiveness and lack of spontaneity are es-
tablished weaknesses in the Japanese character. As long
as Japanese had unrestrioted intercourse with the great in-
dustrial nations and had access to scientifio inventions
and improvements, they were able, by industry and applica-
tion, to copy and make the fullest use of these inventions
and improvements, and the measure of their mastery of them
has been apparent in their fighting in this war. But
American inventive genius will improve on the weapons at
present in use and create new weapons, and the Japanese
beeause of their physical isolation will be unable to learn
of and

=== Page 44 === (confidence 0.96)
-7-
of and eepy these improved and new weapons in time to utilize
them effeetively. It may thus be expeoted that as the war
progresses the Japanese will be fighting with obsolete
weapens, having failed up to the present because of their
lack of originality to perfeot any tool or invent any
mechanical device of importance. Again, everything has gone
well with the Japanese up to the present because they have
been planning and preparing for this war for years, whereas
their enemies obviously had hoped for peace and planned
only defensive action. But we may assume that our general
staffs are devising new strategy and alternative strategy
full of ounning and surprise. If this strategy is not on
the blueprints of the Japanese general staffs we may, be-
cause of the well-knonsterility of the Japanese mind,
count on considerable confusion among the Japanese and
delay in meeting the new situations.
Another charaoteristio of the Japanese is hesitation
and uncertainty in action arising from the practice of dif-
fusing responsibility and making important decisions col-
lestively and after long disoussion. If we can place before
the Japanese a series of new and unexpeeted situations, it
1s not at all unlikely that the Japanese, because of this
habit oflong disoussion and division of responsibility,
will be umable to meet them quickly enough.
B!

=== Page 45 === (confidence 0.99)
-7a-
Here mention may be made of a defect in Japanese
society that oan soaroely fail to make its effeot felt
in a long, all-out war: the abased state of woman in
Japan. Woman's position in society and in the family and
her legal rights are inferior to those of Japanese man,
and by oustom the pursuit of happiness in Japan is only the
man's privilege. Facilities for higher education of women
are inadequate and neglected. Woman is also exoluded from
participation in the affairs of the nation. It would seem
to be a reasonable contention that a nation whose women
have little more than primary education and lead the most
restrioted lives can hardly have the capacity for total
war of a nation whose women are educated, enjoy equality
with men, and are free to contribute their ideas and
abilities to the nation.
Japanese

=== Page 46 === (confidence 0.99)
-8
Japanese national health, as proved by the death rate
of her population, is the poorest among advaneed states.
In l938, the last year for which we have figures, the death
rate per thousand population was 17.4 (in 1924 1t was 21.2,
and in 1931, 19.0). In order to show how high this death
rate is, the death rate per thousand in certain other
oountries 1s given below:

10.7

11.6

14.2

12.1
Although there is believed to be sufficlent food in Japan
for subsistence needs, reports coming out of the country
just before the present war began showed that the publio
health was suffering as a consequence of the rationing
of carbohydrates and proteins and of the abnormally long
working hours. It stands to reason that with a general
low physical vitality to begin with, as evidenced by their
very high death rate, there will be a progressive weakening
of resistance among the Japanese oivilian population to
the inoreasing strain and fatigue and rationing and priva-
tions whieh the war with us will cause.
Oecidental writers who studied the Japanese people as
they were emerging frem their isolation some 50 or 60 years
ago were

=== Page 47 === (confidence 0.99)
ago were struck by the extent of superstition among all
elasses. Time and the 1mpact with the west have mot broughs
abeut any appreciable change in this respect, and Japamese
today are still slaves of superstition, especially in their
private lives and in their household affairs. For instanee,
most Japanese travelers, even men of the world, still are
never without their pocket compass for fear they may lose
their bearings in some new plaoe and sleep lying from north
to south, the position in which corpses are laid, which
will surely result in their early death. Dreams to most
Japanese are still full of the portent of things to come.
Some soothsayers in Japan enjoy national prestige and their
oracles concerning national and international affairs are
respeoted and believed in even by ministers of state. It
1s expeoted that, when things begin to go wrong with the
Japanese, there will be assooiation of misfortune and mis-
haps with forgotten and fanoied omens and that gripping
fear of the supernatural will add to the other factors
that will weaken Japanese morale.
In conolusion a word might be said of an imponderable
and yet serious weakness in the Japanese character: its
deficieney in the sense of abstraot justice, its impercep-
tion of right and wrong. Because of this insensitivity to
true ethiss, the polity of the Japanese people since Japan
omerged

=== Page 48 === (confidence 0.99)
-10
emerged from her seolusion has had no other guides but
material gain, self-interest, and vanity. Thus the history
of Japan's foreign relations in modern times has been almost
an unbroken tale of opportunism, of disregard of and enoroach-
ment on the rights of others, of special concessions exaoted
by force, of seizure and robbery of property, of conquest of
weak neighbors, of commercial monopoly, of absence of true
international cooperation, of abuse of hospitality extended
to her people, of greed, of absence of magnanimity, of deceit
and broken pledges, of commercial exploitation of opium and
narcotio drugs, of unfair trade competition and generally of
all take and no give. At the same time Japan's reoord has
been that of a pushing, vain upstart, striving for front
rank and ciroumstance. "As ye sow so shall ye reap." What
has been the result of Japan's unsorupulous, opportunistic,
olimbing career? Where does Japan stand today in the feelings
of other peoples? Is there a nation so hated by its subjeot
races and neighbors and so generally mistrusted and disliked
by the world at large? We do not even exolude Germany,
Japan's ally for the moment, from Japan's ill-wishers, as
the memory of the spoliation by Japan of their Paoifio
possessions in the World War, of Kaiser Wilhelm II's "Yellow
Peril" theory, and of Japan's role in the Versailles Peace
Conference is still too fresh in the minds of the German
poople

=== Page 49 === (confidence 0.99)
-11-
people for them to have kindly feelings toward
Japan.

THE SECRETARY OF STATE,
WASHING ON. 4/17/43 C3
SIR:
I have the honor to report upon.current conditions
APR:-1943
In Japan as seen by Mr. Ramon LAVALLE, an official of
the Argentine Republic, who has just arrived in Istanbul
from Japan after crossing Siberia to Turkey with his wife
rear.
Mr. Ramon Lavalle was a Special Attaché in the
Argentine Embassy at Tokyo and arrived there at the
beginning of April 1942 from Hong Kong, where he had
been the Argentine Consul. He has been in the Argentine
Foreign Service since 1934, serving in Madrid and Glasgow,
prior to his Hong Kong appointment. He states that he
knows well several American members of the former
Consulate General at Hong Kong and he impresses this
office as being careful and reliable in all his state-
ments. Vice Consul Roy M. Melbourne, who was formerly
asigned to Japan, after several conversations with Nr.
Lavalle believes that the picture he gives of present
gaytions in Japan is essentially correct.
As of further information to the Department there
TIMES AND ADVERTISER, the latest of which is
ecer 8, 1942.
summary of the subjects discussed in this despatch

ven below:

Experiences in Hong Kong.
At the time of the Japanese attack against Hong Kong,
Mr. Lavalle was residing in Kowloon and the account of his
personal experiences and scenes that he witnessed are in
conformity with those related by the Americans evacuated
from Hong Kong upon the exchange vessel ASAMA MARU. At
the end of March Mr. Lavalle and his family, together with
other foreign officials in Hong Kong, were brought by
convoy to Japan. He relates that in Formosa, where the
party remained a week, the Japanese authorities broke all
the official seals and went through every bit of his
package and official consular records despite his protests.
Upon his arrival in Japan, Mr. Lavalle was appointed
a Special Attaché to the Argentine Embassy and remained
in that capacity until his departure from Japan on January
9th.
Attompted Poisoning of Mr. Lavalle:
When Mr. Lavalle reached Tokyo at the beginning of
April his Embassy was naturally very curious to learn the
truth regarding Japanese treatment of civilians and war
prisoners in Hong Kong. He states that he told the
Argentinian Chargé d'Affaires the true story of the brutal
and inhuman treatment suffered by all foreigners in Hong
Kong and on April 4, 1942, the Argentine Embassy despatched
a lengthy code telegram to Buenos Aires upon the subject.
He claims, however, that the codes of the Argentine Republic
1n use in Japan are very old and clearly are known to the
Japanese. On April 5th he had lunch in the Imperial Hotel
in Tokyo with his wife and child and was served identical
food with them, but at the end of the meal he alone took
a cup of tea. One hour later he experienced terrific pains,
fell unconscious, and was brought to a hospital in a most
serious condition, where he remained for a time in grave
danger of death. He was confined to a hospital and to bed
for two months. He was attended by a German Jewish doctor,
who told him privately that after the war and when it was
safe for him to do so, that he would willingly certify that
Mr. Lavalle had been poisoned. He states that even now he
still feels the effects of the ordeal in weak eyesight and
occasional giddiness and general weakness. The case was
immediately taken up by the Argentine Chargé d'Affaires,
who found it necessary to go to the private residence of
the Foreign Minister before he could secure an interview,
and Mr. Lavalle states that a representation in the strongest
terms was made to this Japanese authority, who offered
absolutely no comment or apology.
Current

=== Page 60 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
Current Japanese Morale.
Mr. Lavalle had been in Japan in 1931 and 1932, so
that he was no stranger to the country when he arrived
in April 1942. He was a careful observer and left Japan
with the certain conviction that Japanese public morale
1s completely unshaken and high. The complaints heard
by Americans prior to their departure from Japan of the
food shortage and poor quality of the food, he reports
have been intensified, but that it has no appreciable
effect as yet upon Japanese wartime morale. The Japanese
war communiqués released to the public are still glaringly
successful from the Japanese viewpoint and Japanese are
stated to be forbidden to inquire of foreigners regarding
the trend of Allied war news broadcasts. The only slight
break in the Japanese public's serenity of mind regarding
the progress of war was brought to Mr. Lavalle's attention
when after the so-called Second Naval Battle of the Solomons
and a Japense communiqué that one of their battleships had
been sunk and another damaged, two Japanese employees of
the Argentine Embassy openly asked if this were true and
1f it was given in the Allied news broadcasts, since they
were concerned that Japan had suffered such a loss, which
was the most serious announced up to that date by the
Japanese Navy.
With regard to the attitude of the Japanese people in
this war, Mr. Lavalle verifies that the people are absolute-
ly united in what they consider to be virtually a religious
and racial war and that the Japanese people will never admit
defeat until they are absolutely crushed.
A great fear of the Japanese people, however, is stated
to be Soviet Russia. This is claimed to be steadily growing
and Mr. Lavalle paints the picture of the Japanese scanning
their morning papers anxiously for any report of Japanese-
Soviet clashes. No account of the Russian offensive is said
to have appeared in Japanese newspapers prior to his departure,
but rumor and the very fact that Russia is still resisting
strongly have given rise to grave apprehensions on the part
of Japan with regard to this neighbor.
Dissemination of Allied News.
About four times weekly in Tokyo, Mr. Lavalle reports,
the Argentine Chargé d'Arfaires and the Spanish Minister
combine to put out a wartime news bulletin containing
interesting items from the Allied broadcasts. This bulletin
is made in about twenty copies and is most discreetly
distributed to prominent foreigners and to some Japanese.
From these persons the news continues by word of mouth. The
Spanish Minister is said to present one copy to Mrs. Sawada,
the wife of the former Japanese Ambassador to France, whoin
the privacy of her own home types a few copies and distributes
them to some American nisei.
General

=== Page 61 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
Goneral Economic Conditions.
The Japanese Government is reported to be still
adhering to its officially sponsored low-price policy
in order to avoid inflation within the country. How-
ever, this office's informant states that prices never-
theless are slowly rising and that the Japanese working
class are securing more money and are "prosperous" for
their scale of living. A typical lunch at Lohmeyer's
restaurant or the Imperial hotel in Tokyo costs between
2 1/2 and 3 1/2 yen and dinner in these same establish-
ments costs between 5 1/2 and 6 yen. There is still the
same shortage of commodities available to the general
public and this has become accentuated with the increasing
difficulties of transport from overseas.
The subject of Japanese overseas transport is covered
in a separate report, No. 6 or March 2, 1943, entitled
"Japanese Wartime Shipping: June 1942 to January 1943."
However, in connection with this despatch the statement
of a prominent Osaka Shosen Kaisha official to Mr. Lavalle
is again worth quoting here. The Japenese stated that
business leaders in the country realized that sufficient
ocean tonnage transport was fundamental for a Japanese
victory, that Japan could not possibly construct the
needed tonnage, and that necessarily Japan would lose
the war.
Because of the scarcity of industrial materials,
with the lack of adequate transport, Japanese industry
is said to be experiencing difficulties in maintaining
a full wartime production schedule. There are claimed
to be large quantities of materials at overseas points
piled high in warehouses and in docks awaiting shipment
to Japan, which is impossible because of the shipping
shortage.
Treatment or Foreigners.
From Mr. Lavalle's observations it appears that the
Japanese are not favoring any foreigners now residing in
Japan, inoluding their allies, Germany and Italy. He
states that there is a coldness between the Germans and
Japanese and that the Italians are little respected by
them. The French residents in Japan he believes to be
the worst treated and to be subjected to many indignities,
from which their mbassador is not exoluded. The Germans
are seid to have a communal food commissary system for
their mationals, but the other nationals are not said to
have been able to organize their food distribution system
on such a basis.
The Soviet Embassy in Tokyo is stated to be most
carefully guarded by the Japanese and officials of the
Embassy are closely shadowed wherever they go. Never-
theless it is Mr. Lavalle's contention, based upon
personal

=== Page 62 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
personal relations with the Russians, that they are able
to secure a quantity of useful information to a greater
extent than the representatives of any other country now
in Tokyo.
When Mr. Lavalle left Japan, the country was in the
throes of one of its periodic anti-spy campaigns, which
was pushed with great vigor by the authorities. Since
the war it has been impossible for any Japanese officially
to mingle with foreigners and although invitations are
accepted by them, at the last minute they are always
cancelled through illness or some similar excuse.
The Swiss Authorities.
Mr. Lavalle states that the Swiss authorities in
Japan, charged with the protection of Allied interests,
are not greatly respected by the Japanese. He states
as positive knowledge that the Swiss Minister is considered
pro-Axis and pro-Japanese and is said to have remcrked
to the Argentine Chargé d'Affaires when the latter first
turned over British interests to the Swiss, that "there
would be trouble for about two years until Japan won the
war." Mr. Robert HAUSHEER, known to all those interned
in the American and British Embassies in Tokyo as the
Swiss Third Secretary who was of the greatest assistance
to them, was forced to resign his position as a result
of a disagreement with his Minister on the subject of
protection of Allied interests, and is now living private-
ly in Kobe. When questioned upon the attitude adopted by
the Swiss Legation in Tokyo after the report of the "suicide"
of Mr. BOSSERT, who had been sent to Formosa in behalf of
American interests, Mr. Lavalle stated that the Swiss
Minister issued strict instructions to all Swiss nationals
not to discuss the case, and, although he is not certain,
some Swiss informed him that they do not believe the real
account of the incident had been reported to Switzerland.
Treatment or United Nations' Nationals.
It is related that the condition of United Nations'
civilians interned in Japan is considered satisfactory
by the Swiss, although no other foreign representatives
in Japan are naturally in a position to verify this. Mr.
Lavalle, however, relates seeing in November l942 a group
of 12 Canadian and British soldiers arriving at a Yokohama
railroad station in such a pitiable state of malnutrition
that it was necessary for the Japanese guards to assist
them in walking.
The nisei in Japan are claimed to be closely watched
and are not trusted. The story was reliably given to Mr.
Lavalle of a young nisei couple, who one evening nostalgically
played American music on their victrola and sang some
Lmerican songs. Their house was entered by the police,
thedr

=== Page 63 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
their records were broken and they were severely beaten.
Economically also conditions are most difficult for them.
On the other hand, since there is such a great need for
persons of Japanese race who speak English in the southern
occupied areas, the Greater East Asia Ministry is said to
be seeking to use numbers of nisei in this capacity. An
influential Japanese, who is claimed to do everything
possible to assist the nisei, is Mrs. Sawada, the wife
of the former ambassador to France.
Arrests of Japanese.
The Japanese authorities are stated to have arrested
at least 10 professors of Keio and Imperial Universities
during the autumn of 1942, apparently because of some
disagreement with Government policies. Additionally, at
a slightly later time, some university students also were
reported to have been arrested. This may be used as an
indication that although the Japanese public is solidly
behind its present military leaders, there are elements
of dissidence among intellectual and industrial circles
that require suppression by those in power. Mr. Lavalle
states that he has heard of several thinking and prominent
Japanese who have expressed their complete disagreement
with the course adopted by the military authorities in
this war.
Shins Exoloded in Yokohama Harbor.
Towards the end of November 1942, there was a terrific
series of explosions in Yokohama harbor, followed by a
large conflagration that destroyed six vessels that were
moored at the main wharf in Yokohama near the American
Consulate General building there. Explosions continued
all day and warehouses on the dock and buildings surrounding
the area were particularly shaken and all glass in the
buildings in the waterfront area were broken. Three days
later Mr. Lavalle visited Yokohama and the fires were still
burning. He states that the most reliable sources have
indicated that the conflagration involved the formerly
British vessel NANKING, which had been converted into a
raider, 2 German raiders, 1 of 10,000 and the other of
12,000 tons, 2 Japanese freighters or raiders and 1
Japanese tanker.  The vessels were reported to have been
loading ammunition, in the course of which an accident
ocourred, which resulted in the ultimate destruction of
the 6 ships. The Japanese tanker was said to have been
supplying these vessels with oil at the time and thus
added additional fuel to the conflagration. Announced
casualties were go Germans killed upon the 2 raiders,
and 160 wounded. l00 coolies also were reported to have
been killed.
The Removal of the Garman Ambassador.
The story that is told in official foreign eircles
1n

=== Page 64 === (confidence 0.99)
-7 -
in Tokyo and which is said to be generally believed
regarding the removal of the former German Ambassador, General
Eugen OTT, is stated by this office's informant to be due
to difriculties in which the Ambassador's son reportedly
was involved with Gestapo agents at the German Embassy.
Two German correspondents were said to have been acoused
by the Gestapo of being agents of a foreign power. In
the course of their conrinement in the German Embassy, the
Ambassador's son, who allegedly knew one of the accused
men quite well, attempted to intercede actively for him.
This was discovered by the Gestapo agents, according to
a report and there were serious difficulties during the
summer of 1942, which finally resulted in the Ambassador's
son leaving Japan in September upon a German blockade
runner, which was bound for Germany with supplies and
other Germans to join the army. The Ambassador reportedly
protested to Berlin regarding the whole affair and received
no satisfaction, except that the difficulties then brought
to a head resulted in his removal as ambassador.
Communications Between Japan and the Outside World.
The Argentine Embassy in Tokyo is stated to have no
communication with Buenos Aires except by telegram and
telephone. No attempts are made by this Embassy to send
letters or other communications to its Government. Mr.
Lavalle did not bring any official Argentine communications
with him, in view of his travel through Soviet Russia,
since his Government had no established relations with
that country and since he was traveling at his own risk.
The Swiss Legation also is unable to send out any mail
from Japan and Mr. Lavalle reportedly brought with him
19 letters from the Swiss ofricials in Tokyo. In the
month of December 1942 the Swedish Minister received a
letter from his country dated July, and this was said to
be quite normal.
It thus can be seen that mail communications between
Japan and outside countries are extremely slow and difficult.
However, the sole exception to these difficult communications
is the Soviet Russian Embassy, which reportedly has a
regular monthly courier service to and from Japan via
Korea or Dairen to Manchuria and the Siberian border.
Communications with Germany.
Communications with Germany are said to be kept open
by blockade runners of the two countries which travel
between the Far Bast and Europe by circuitous routes.
Mr. Lavalle states that he believes these ships leave
Japan carrying official German communications and men
for the army at the rate of one a month and that in
Kobe a German raider appears for refitting and supply
on an average of once a month.
Ra11

=== Page 65 === (confidence 0.99)
-8-
Rail Communications in Japan.
It 1s stated that rail transport has noticeably
increased since the outbreak of the war and that severe
restrictions are now imposed upon civilian passenger
traffic of all kinds. As an example of the way the
authorities discourage rail trafric, Mr. Lavalle reports
that during the New Year's holidays all first and second
class coaches, dining cars and sleepers, were removed
and that only third class coaches were run throughout
the country.
Departure from Japan.
Because of his difficulty with the Japanese authorities,
Mr. Lavalle believed that it would be exceedingly trying
to leave Japan. Nevertheless arrangements were made through
the Argentine Embassy for him to proceed via Soviet Russia.
Through the Soviet Embassy it was possible for him to secure
a Soviet transit visa, although the Argentine Republic had
no diplomatic relations with Soviet Russia. When his plans
matured it was necessary for Mr. Lavalle to sign a statement
made by the Argentine Embassy that he was traveling through
Soviet Russia at his own risk and without the protection
of his own Government. However, the Soviet Embassy gave
him an introductory letter in the form of "To Whom It May
Concern", which requested all Soviet Government officials
to extend him every possible courtesy.
Permission was secured from the Japanese to leave the
Japanese Empire and arrangements were made for Mr. Lavalle
and his family to proceed to Siberia via Korea. At the
last minute the Japanese told him that it was impossible
to proceed by that way for military reasons. After the
usual bickerings and delays common to Japanese authorities
when they are performing an unwilling service, Mr. Lavalle
and his family were requested to be ready within a day to
go to Kobe and take ship for Dairen. This time limit was
unexpeotedly moved up, until actually Mr. Lavalle and his
family had one hour's notice to catch the train for Kobe,
and he surprised the Japanese authorities by being able to
be ready within that time limit. He and his family left
Kobe on January 9th and the trip from Kobe by boat was
made under most severe blackout restrictions and was ac-
companied by the strictest precautions against Allied sub-
marines and was not particularly pleasant. During the
journey, in fact until he crossed the Manchurian-Siberian
border, Mr. Lavalle carried a pistol on his person at all
times, since he naturally was not certain what aggressive
acts the Japanese might attempt against him and his family
during the course of the trip. The trip was made by rail
to Manchouli and Mr. Lavalle was greatly relieved when he
crossed into Russian territory on January 17th.
Travel Through Siberia.
In his travel with his family through Siberia, Mr.
Lavalle

Siberian Observations.
The treatment accorded Mr. Lavalle and his family
throughout his stay in Soviet Russia was said to be
IN THE
excellent. The introductory letter from the Soviet
Embassy at Tokyo was most useful to him and he experienced
no difficulties traveling upon his Argentine official
passport. He stated that throughout the trip upon all
the railroads there were definite signs of war activity,
with shipments of freight and troops being freguently
encountered. The family diet throughout the trip consisted
BULKY FILE
mainly of canned sardines, but he reported a welcome change
at Krasnovosk on the Caspian Sea, where they were proudly
served some American canned goods by the local hotel.
Spirit with regard to the war was high in every district
in which he traveled, and because of rail deleys he was
in some localities for several days and talked freely to
those who spoke any foreign language.

olouds.
America, whioh is the leading country of the

pretty nearly all the countries that have become great
have used their greatness, that is, their power, for an
unworthy end, either in pampering luxury or in conquering

oations, it seems to me, that your country is not follow-

extended for one year. On February 18, 1942, however,
the Japanese Cabinet decided to hold a "drastio reforma-
tion eleotion" this year, differing from the old-style
elections in order to promote completion of the so-called
Greater East Asia War. Only candidates familiar with
Greater East Asin problems would be considered acceptable
to the Government, it was announoed. As politioal parties
were disbanded in 1940, some substitute method of placing
candidates before the voters had to be employed. The
system devised was the setting up within the Imperial Rule
Assistance Association of a nominating council, appointed
by the Association president, General Nobuyuki Abe (former
Prime Min1ster, August 30, 1939 to January 15, 1940), the
sole function of which was to select and recommend suitable
candidates for Diet seats.
An election was held as scheduled on April 30, 1942 and,
according to Ambassador Grew's despatch of August 19, 1942,
381 of the successful candidates had been recommended by the
counoil. This figure represents approximately 82 per cent
of the total membership (466) of the Lower House. In this
connection Ambassador Grew's despatch of August 19 remarks
that "although these results represented an overwhelming
viotory for the Council's candidates, there was considerable
shaking of heads on the part of the people over this system
of 'officially recommended' candidates". That despatch also
mentions thet practioally every prominent leader of extreme
rightist affiliation, including many with police records of
implication in crimes of political violence, appears in the
list of successful candidates.
The offioial attitude toward the elections was expressed
in a pre-election Diet speech by Premier Tojo, excerpts from
which were quoted in a Tokyo broadoast of March 26. The
Prime Minister was reported to have denied charges of undue
Government meddling with the election process, adding that
"the candidates will be elected by the people according to
the method we think right". He announoed that there would
be no eleotion campaign and the radio report of his speech
closed with the ambiguous threat, "We will enforce punishment
of those who make such statements as bring unnecessary sus-
picion on people engaged in the war".

Abolition of the "Parliamentary Vice Miniater and Councilor
system".
A Tokyo broadoast of June 2 announced that, in order
to promote greater efficienoy, committees within the vari-
ous Ministries of the Japanese Cabinet had been formed,
composed of members of the House of Peers, the House of
Representatives and selected scholars. "With the formation
of these committees in each Ministry, the present parlia-
mentary Vice Minister and councilor system will be abolished."
(Since l924 a parliamentary vice minister and a parliamentary
counselor have been appointed to each ministry for the
ostensible purpose of maintaining liaison between the gov-
ernment and the houses of the Diet. There was thus a vice
minister and a counselor, one for the House of Peers and
another for the House of Representatives, attached to each
Ministry. The occupants of these positions ohanged with
the Cabinet.)
Administratora for Occupied Arean
In order to release administrative officials for ser-

Colonial Government
The Ministry of Greater East Asiatic Affairs was
created on November 1, 1942 to supervise all Japanese
aotivities, other than military and naval, in occupied
territories, including Manchuria. (The present disoussion
is limited to Japan and the Empire and therefore no attempt
1s made here to desoribe the activities of the new Ministry
in their relation to foreign territory under Japanese mili-
tary occupation or control. A memorandum of November 5,
prepared in FE, treats of this new Ministry.) Some sweep-
ing administrative changes were effeoted in the Japanese
internal and colonial administrative system concurrently
with and, at least partially, as a result of the estab-
lishment of the new Ministry. Perhaps the most striking
development was the abolition of the Ministry of Overseas
Affairs, which was established in l929 and whioh supervised
the administrative work of the Chosen and Taiwan Governments-
General, the Kwantung and Karafuto Governments, and the busi-
ness operations of the South Manohuria Railway Company and
the Oriental Development Company.
From radio reports from Tokyo it is possible to piece
together a general outline of the changes to which the
oreation of a new and the abolition of an old ministry will
give rise.
Kxantung Leased Territory. It is learned from a Tokyo
broadoast of September 1 that all matters pertaining to the
Kwantung

=== Page 81 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
Kwantung Bureau now fall under the jurisdiction of the
Ministry of Greater East Asiatio Affairs. Since Manohuria
is inoluded in the new Ministry's sphere, it seems logical
that the Kwantung Bureau as well, which funotions under
the control of the Japanese Ambassador at Hsinking, is
also under the supervision of the new Ministry. (The
Kwantung Bureau was established on December 26, 1934 at
Hsinking under the control of the Japanese Ambassador there
and most of the administrative powers formerly vested in
the Government of the Kwantung Leased Territory were trans-
ferred to the President of the Bureau.)
Karafuto. According to a Tokyo broadoast of Septem-
ber 1, "Karafuto shall be included in the administration
of Japan proper". The Tokyo radio stated on September 1l
that Karafuto "shall be inoluded within the jurisdiction
of the Home Ministry, instead of the Overseas Ministry as
at present". It appears from the foregoing that Karafuto
1s to be placed within the administrative system of Japan
proper and it is therefore assumed that, as in the Hokkaido,
1te government will approximate that of the Jepanese pre-
feotures.
Chosen and Taiwan. Chosen and Taiwan heretofore have
been administered by governments-general, which, except
for certain supervisory powers vested in the Ministry of
Overseas Affairs, operated independently of the administra-
tive system of Japan proper. It now appears, however, that
steps are being taken to integrate them more closely into
the administrative system of Japan. The Ministry of Home
Affaire, which supervises the prefectural governments of
Japan proper, henceforth will exercise supervisory funotions
in Chocen and Taiwan which previously appertained to the
now defunct Overseas Ministry. Tokyo broadoast on Septem-
ber 11:
"Problems 1n connection with the unifioation of
various problems within and without Japan proper and
control of business affairs of the Chosen Government
General, the Taiwen Government General and the Karafuto
Government Office shall be under the Home Ministry.
For the convenience of administrative purposes, the
Superintendents' Bureau of the Home Ministry shall be
oreated and its personnel shall be appointed mostly
from the personnel of the Overseas Ministry and the
officials of....Bureau. A co-ordination committee
shall be created in the Home Ministry to bring about
a close relationship between various appropriate
bureaus concerning Japan proper, Chosen, Taiwan and
Karafuto."

=== Page 82 === (confidence 0.98)
-8
An interesting innovation under the new aystem is
provision for the extension of the jurisdiction of the
several ministries in the Japanese Cabinet to the affairs
of Chosen and Taiwan. Heretofore administretion of Chosen
and Taiwan has been organized on a basis distinct and
separate from Japan proper but the broadoast of September 1l
states thet in Chosen "business affairs, according to their
nature, shall be administered by the Premier or the proper
Minister". The Taiwen Government-General, according to the
same broadoast, "shall be controlled by the Premier or the
proper Minister, according to the nature of. the business
involved, according to the regulations, and the jurisdictions
of these Ministries, restricted under the present law, shall
be expanded". While it is indicated that the administration
of both Chosen and Taiwan will reamin in the hands of their
respective Governments-General, it may be assumed that the
authority and independence of those Governments-General will
be curtailed as a result of the participation of the ministries
of the central government in colonial administration. The
supervisory functions of the several Japanese Cabinet ministers
to be exercised in Chosen and Taiwan are enumerated in the
monitored report of an Information Board announcement of
September 1l, as follows:
"The parts of the Chosen and Taiwan Governments-
General to be supervised by the Premier or respective
ministers are as follows: A--Army conscription by the
Premier. B--Taxation, currenoy and population matters
by the Finance Minister. C--Education problems con-

communication, sea transportation, coastal navigation
and transportation by the Commerce and Industry Ministry.
G--The railways by the Railway Ministry. H-Foreign
trade by the Finance Minister and the Commeroe and
Industry Minister."
Ministry of Foreign Affaira
The Tokyo radio reported an important reorganization
of the Japanese Foreign Orrice, effeotive November 1, 1942.
The outstanding feature of this reorganization was the
relinquishment by the Foreign Office of its important
eoonom1e

=== Page 83 === (confidence 0.98)
-9-
•conomic, political and cultural functions in Japanese-
occupied territories and the delegation of those funotions
to the newly oreated Ministry of Greater East Asiatio
Affairs. Only matters of a "purely diplomatio nature"
will be left within the jurlsdiotion of the Foreign Office
in these areas.
As now constituted, the Foreign Office consists of
only four bureaus, namely, Political Affairs, Trade,
Treaties and Research. Aithough information concerning
the exact functions of these bureaus 1s lacking, it is
reported that the Bureau of Political Affairs has absorbed
the Bureaus of European, Asiatic and American Affairs and
also has taken over "diplomatic affairs" relating to
"Greater East Asia", previously handled by the Bureaus of
East Asiatic and South Seas Affairs. These latter two
bureaus have been transferred to the Ministry of Greater
East Asiatic Affairs.
An interesting sidelight on this diminution of the
powers of the Foreign Office is the resignation of Foreign
Minister Togo, "for purely personal reasons", on the same
day (September 1) that the first announcement was made of
plans to create the Ministry of Greater East Asiatio Affairs,
and the subsequent appointment to that post of Masayuk1
Tani whose principal distinction has been his close assoc1-
ation in recent years with the Japanese military program
in China.

of Chosen and Taiwan. Heretofore administration of Chosen
and Taiwen has been organised on a basis distinet and
separate from Japan proper but the broadoast of September 11
states that in Chosen "business affnirs, according to their
nature, shall be administered by the Premier or the proper
Minister". The Taiwan Government-General, according to the
same broadeast, "shall be controlled by the Premier or the
proper Minister, according to the nature of the bueiness
involved, according to the regulations, and the jurisdietions
of these Ministries, restrioted under the present law, shall
be expanded". While it is indicated thst the administration
of both Chosen and Taiwan will reamin in the hands of their
respective Governments-Deneral, it may be assumed that the
authority and independence of those Governments-General will
be eurtailed as a result of the partioipation of the ministries
of the central government in colonial administration. The
supervisory funotions of the several Japanese Cabinet ministers
to be exeroised in Chosen and Taiwan are enumerated in the

The Japanese Government in making the recent changes in
the Japanese Cabinet was probably motivated largely by a desire
to take up all slack in internal administration, to take ad-
vantage of the most competent administrators available, to
bring about greater unity in home affairs and in foreign and
domestic propaganda, and to facilitate consolidation of Japan's
position and prosecution of the w r. In reneral, it is believed
that the new appointments  tne ohinet do, in fact, represent
o would not repeat
a strengthening of t umese overmen.
not sean to suggest deviation in basic policies.
Shigemitsu, who nas served as ambassador in Soviet Russia,
in England, and in China, is probably better qualified than any
other statesman in Japan to deal with a wide range of problems in
foreign affairs such as Axis relations, soviet neutrality, rela-
tions with puppets and possible future peace overtures. He aided
the inauguration last November of Japan's UOTE soft UNQUOTE polioy
toward China and should be more acceptable to the puppet Chinese
than his predecessor. While not especially popular with Soviet
officlals it is believed that he is not repeat not disliked by
the Soviet Government. Shigemitsu has on previous occasions let
it be known that he favors completely Japan's domination of East
Asia.
