MORANDUM TO MR. VINCENT REGARDING CONVERSATION WITH MR.
SHAO YU-LIN IN CONNEOTION WITH THE RECENT CABINET CHANGES
IN JAPAN
Mr. Sheo Yu-lin, director of the Department of Inform-
ation or the Mihistry for Foreign Affairs, opened the inter-
view by saying that he had a lengthy telegram prepared con-
cerming the recent cabinet changes in Japan which he was
imterested in sending to Mr. T. V. Soong at Washington.
This cable apparently represented the views of the Ministry
for Foreign Affairs. Ar. Shao suggested that I take the
cable and have the Embassy send it to the Department for
its ewn information and also to make the material avail-
able to Mr. Soong. Ireplied that, while personally I
sew no objection to this proposal, I would naturally have
to refer the matter to you and suggested that we get in
toueh with you by telephone.
Mr. Shao then seemed to lose interest in his proposal
and said he would give his own views on the sub jeot of the
Japanose oabinet changes. He spoke for more than 45 minutes
and, as I was leaving, pointed out that he had spoken muoh
more frankly to me than he had to newsnaper reporters. He
intimated that the material given me was substantially what
he had proposed sending to Mr. Soong.
Mr. Shao devoted the entire interview to the appoint-
mont of Shigemitsu as Foreign Minister in Tokyo. He dis-
missed the other appointments as having local significance
only.
According to Mr. Shac, there have recently been evi-
dences of Japanese and German efforts to alienate Russia
from the United Nations. The Axis has attempted to make
capital, for example, out of Ambassador Standley's reported
statement concerning Russia's lack of appreciation of
Amorioan Lend-Lease aid, out of the atrocity story con-
cerning the massacre of Polish army offieers by the Russians,
and out of the Polish-Russian border dispute.
In Mr. Shao's opinion, much significance should be
attached to the fact that Mr. Goro Morishima, Japanese
Minister to Kuibyshev assisting the Japanese Ambassador
there, was reoalled to Tokyo last December and, according
to Japanese radio broadoasts, had a long talk with Premier
Tojo a few days ago. "Morishima is an expert in intrigue",
Mr. Shao said. "His return to Japan and his reoent talk
wd th Tojo naturally bring up the question: why did he have
to return to Japan for such a long visit? It ould be only
beeeuse he was on extraordinary business", he added.
Mr. Sheo declared that, with the exoeption of the fish-
eries negotiations, whioh have been amicably settled for
another year, there is now no important business to be
negotiated botween Japan and Soviet Russia. With the fisheries
question settled, he pointed out, one is foroed to conolude
that there must be something signifioant under disoussion
between Japan and Russia. In this conneotion, Mr. Shao said
it is "wieely known" that Sato, the Japanese Ambassador to
Ru

=== Page 108 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
waia, has been attempting to bring about peace between
Germany and Russia and that he is the source of numerous
rumors concerning such a peaee.
After giving this background of Japanese attempts
to arrive at a rapprochement with Russia and to aot as
go-between in negottating peace between Russia and Germany,
Mr. Sheo took up the subjeot of Shigemitsu's appeintment.
Coneerning Shigemitsu himself, Mr. Shao said that he was
a man of two personalities. In the first place, he "seems
to be a liberal, possibly pro-British and in this charmster
he also seems to be anti-Bolshevist". In the secend place,
however, he was the former Japanese ambassador to Russia
and thus knows somothing about Russia. Thus, Mr. Shao
pointed out, he is an etcellent man to throw a smoke sereen
over Japanese diplomatic moves.
According to Mr. Shao, the best possible policy for
Japan now is to reach an understanding with Russia. Japan
will try first to get such an understanding by politioal
means or pressure. Failing in this maneuver, whieh in-
volves the negotiation of a Russo-German peace, Japan
"may take astion when Germany starts on its summer offen-
sive against Russia", he said.
Thus Shigomitsu is the logical choioe, both for carry-
ing on these negotiations and also to carry on if Japan
does attack Russia. In that event, Mr. Shao explained,
shigemitsu and the Japanese will set themselves up as
leaders in the fight against Communism and will even try
to ealist support from the United States and Great Britain
for this policy. In other words, Mr. Shao said, the Japanese
would make the claim that they are fighting the Boisheviks
and Communism as an excuse for war against the Soviet Union.
Mr. Shao was of the opinion that Shigemitsu's activities
while Japanese mbassader to the Nanking regime, during which
me he brought about Nanking's deelaration of war on the
United States and Great Britain, may be linked up with present
and future developments insofar as he (Shigemitsu) is con-
cerned. In other words, he succeeded in lining up the Nanking
regime's support for the general fight for the Co-Prosperity
Sphere and also, if necessary, for the ooming fight with
Russia. This having been done, his next move was to return
to Tokyo and direet negotiations wien Russia as indicated.
In foousing attention on the other end of the Axis
again, Mr. Shao said that Germany has recently made great
use of the "uneomditional surrender" statements put forth
by leaders of the United Nations. The people in Germany
have been teld ef these statements and have been prepared
for certain developments, the most favorable (for Germany)
being a. separate peaee with Russia whieh would permit her
te face Britaim and the United States without fear of the
easterm freat and Russia. Japan, would alse like to have
massia as a pemeeful neighberlam least for the time being,
se she could dotote her fuil attention to other areas in
the Paeifie.
Mr. sheo was very serieus in making his coneluding
tat：

=== Page 109 === (confidence 0.98)
-3
tatement: "Ir we allow this to be realized (German-
Tmmemese rapproehement with Russia) the entire world
tuation will be ohanged. We, Chine and the United
States, must find some way to prevent this from happen-
ing, openly or secretly", he said.
At the end of the interview I questioned Mr. Sheo
about the telegram he had prepared for Mr. Soong and asked
if he would sare to make a copy of it available to the
mbassy, even if it was decided not to send it to Washing-
ton. He said that he had changed his mind about sending
it to Mr. Soong, who could get the ideas outlined above in
a brief telegram.

PRANDOM TO HR. VIHORNT REGARDING CONVSRSATION WITH MR.
YU-LIN IN CONNECTION WITH THE RECRNT CABINET CHANGES
IN JAPAN
 Mr. Sheo Yu-lin, director of the Department of Inform-
ation of the Ministry for Foreign Affairs, opened the inter-
vlew by saying that he had a lengthy telegram prepared ocon-
varnins the recent cabinet changes in Japan which he was
Imterested in sending to lir. T. V. Soong at Washington.
This cable apparently represanted the views of the Ministry
for Foreign Affairs. Mr. Shao suggested that I take the
cable and have the Embassy send it to the Department for
its oym information and also to make the material avail-
able to Hr. Soong. I replied that, while personally I
saw no objeotion to this proposal, I would naturally have
to refer the matter to you end suggested that we get in
touch with you by telephone.
Mr. Shao then seamed to lose interest in his proposal
and said he would give his own views on the subjeot of the
Japanese cabinet changes. He spoke for more than 45 minutes
and, as I was leaving, pointed out that he had spoken much
more frankly to me than he had to newspaper reporters. He
intimated that the material given me was substantially vhat
he had proposed sending to Mr. Soong.
Mr. Shao devoted the entire interview to the appoint-
ment of Shigemitau as Foreign Minister in Tokyo. He dis-
missed the other appointments as having local significance
only.
According to Mr. Shao, there have recently been evi-
dences of Japanese and German efforts to alienate Russia
from the United Nations. The Axis has attempted to make
capital, for example, out of Ambassador Standley's reported
statement conoerning Russia's lack of appreciation of
Amerioan Lend-Lease aid, out of the atrocity story con-
cerning the massaore of Polish army officers by the Russians,
and out of the Polish-Russian border dispute.
In Mr. Shao's opinion, much significance should be
attaohed to the fact that Mr. Goro Ilorishima, Japanese
Minister to Kuibyshev assisting the Japanese Ambassador
there, was recalled to Tokyo last December and, according
to Japanese radio broadoasts, had a long talk wi th Premier
Tojo a few days ago. "Vorishima is an expert in intrigue",
Mr. Shao said. "His return to Japan and his recent talk
d th Tojo neturally bring up the question: why did he have
to return to Japan for suoh a long visit? It could be only
beasuse he was on extraordinary business", he added.
Mr. Sheo deolared that, with the exoeption of the fish-
eries negotiations, whioh have been amioably settled for
amother year, there is now no important business to be
negotlated between Japan and Soviet Russia. With the fisheries
mestion settled, he pointed out, one is forced to conclude
thet there must be something signifioant under disoussion
between Japan and Russis. In this conneotion, Mr. Shao said
It is "widely knawm" thet Bato, the Japanese Ambassador to
Russia

Germany and Russia and that he is the souroe of numerous
rumors concerning such a peace.
After giving this background of Japanese attempts
to arrive at a rapproohement with Russia and to act as
go-between in negotiating peace between Russia and Germany,
Mr. Shao took up the subject of Shigemitsu's appointment.
Concerning Shigemitsu himself, Mr. Shao said that he was
a man of two personalities. In the first place, he "seems
to be a liberal, possibly pro-British and in this charaoter
he also seems to be anti-Bolshevist". In the second plaoe,
however, he was the former Japanese ambassador to Russia

According to Mr. Shao, the bost possible polioy for

wiil try first to get such an understanding by political
means or pressure. Failing in this maneuver, which in-
volves the negotiation of a Russo-German peace, Japan
"may take action when Germany starts on its summer offen-
sive against Russia", he said.
Thus Shigemitsu 1s the logical choice, both for carry-

Shigemitsu and the Japenese will set themselves up as

and Communiam as an exouse for war against the Soviet Union.
Mr. Shao was of the opinion that Shigemitsu's aotivities
while Japanese ambassador to the Nanking regime, during which
time he brought about Nanking's deolaration of war on the
United States and Great Britain, may be linked up with present
and future developments insofar as he (Shigemitsu) is oon-
oerned. In other words, he suoceeded in lining up the Nanking
regime's support for the general fight for the Co-Prosperity
Sphere and also, if necessary, for the coming fight with
Russia. This having been done, his next move was to return
to Tokyo and direct negotiations with Russia as indicated.
In foousing attention on the other end of the Axis
again, Nr. Shao said that Germany has recently made great
use or the "unconditional surrender" statements put forth
by leaders of the United Nations. The people in Germany
have been told of these statements and have been prepared
for certain developments, the most favorable (for Germany)
being a separate peace with Russia whioh would permit her
to face Britain and the United States without fear of the
eastern front and Russia. Japan would also like to have
Russia as a peaceful neighbor, at least for the time being,
so she could devote her full attention to other areas in
the Paoirio.
Mr. Shao was very merious in making his conoluding

of the Imperial Assistance Political Association while Tojo
is its direotor conourrently; vamasaki is chief of general
affairs of the Assistance Political Association, and Qasa
is the phief or general affairs of Imperial Assistance
Politlcal Association. As these people heve now been
indluded in the cabinet, it is possible that they may
arrive at a compromise with each other and reorganize
ehemeelves.
Summarising the above four points, we feel that the
reergmnisation of the Tojo cabinet has a great bearing on
Fom the reorganization of the Tojo cabinet it was
atated that externally the cabinet will promote Axis
relattens and that internally it will cooperate with the

msreneth may be uniried. This is honest talk. It is thus

I have the honor
 report upon the result of an
interview with a member of the Turkish diplomatic party
that left Japan on April lst of this year and reached
Istanbul on May l4th. In deference to the ofrioe's
source, his name is not mentioned, but he may be
deemed thoroughly reliable, since he has lived for
many years in Japan and appeared thoroughly aoquainted
with conditions in the country. In general, his ob-
servations confirm the information previously submit-
tod to the Department in despaton No. 223 of Maroh 3,
1943 entitled "A Roport on Current Conditions in Japan."
This most recent source, however, was able to bring
these observations up to the point of his departure
in April, while the prior information geased in January.
894.00/76
Japanese Morale:
PS/RGB
Japanese public morale is still very high and the
people appear most optimistic and determined, since
they feel in a most fundamental sense that it is a
question of the defeat of the Allies or the complete
erushing of their country by its enemies. Aside from
the unthinking mass of the people, who hear absolutely
no information from the Allied side, because of the
extremely stringent Japanese censorship measures that
were perfeeted prior to the wutbreak of the war, thete
are elements amons she educatoa and thimrins people in
the
KETILI ANU BENABILITATION

=== Page 124 === (confidence 0.99)
the country who openly in conversation are expressing
their doubts regarding the possibility of ultimate
success. These people claim that Japan at te outbreak
of the war adopted a blitzkrieg system of attaok which
assured quick and, early suocesses, but they now eomment
that viotories appear very slow in coming and that,
after all, the United States and Great Britain are
most powerful nations and have immense resouroes to
draw upon. Although the authorities ban the use of
radios by Japanese subjects, whenever a foreigner
wishes to dispose of a short-wave radio or if he plans
to leave, several interested Japanese privately express
their wish to buy the radio for whatever price the
seller wishes.
Propaganda Methods:
Japanese propaganda methods used to stimulate
hatred of the United States and Great Britain are said
to involve the placing of large caricaturés ana cartoons
of Allied figures along such public places as the Ginza
in Tokyo, with these figures sometimes being represented
as being heavily pummelled by Japanese. Another device
is to draw the American flag or the British Union-Jack
on heavily frequented street corners, suoh as the main
intersections on the Ginza, in order that the Japanese
may figuratively step upon these flags. The anti-
Amerioan and anti-British propaganda campaign is in
full tide and greatly exploited by the authorities
for their war purposes.
Rolations with Germans and Italians:
The Japanese are stated to be on better terms with
the Germans than with the nationals of any other Axis
states. The Germans are extremely useful to Japan in
the country's war effort and henoe, are respeoted for
this. However, the Italians are belittled by the Japa-
nese, who seem to care nothing for them and refer
contomptuously to Mussolini as being nothing but a
mouth-piece for Hitler.
Preparation for Air-raids:
Since the Amorioan air-raid upon Japan in April 1942,
the traveler states that the Japanese have become very
cautious and have intensified their air-raid precautions
in every respect. Surprise blackouts are held at irre-
gular intervals, as well as surprise daylight air-raid
drills, with the effeot of allegedly bringing air-raid
preeautions to a point whereby emergenoy personnel are
in action within a comparatively short time and a total
blaskeut is possible most rapidly. No additions have
boon
10177

=== Page 125 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
besn made to anti-airraid technique or emergeney personmol
organisation and implements supplied to them, which are
already familiar to the Department.
Army Mobilization:
Mobilization for the army is olaimed to continue
at the normal pace prevailing prior to the war with
the United States. At certain intervals classes are
called for service without publicity and every effort
is made to place this action upon a most quiet, almost
secretive, basis.
No Material Evidence of the Occupation of the
Southern Regions:
In securing oommodities there is stated to be little
evidence to the general public of the raw materials made
available to the country by the occupation of the southern
regions. The traveler stated that all commodities from
the south that do arrive are completely utilized by
industry in the war effort and, consequently, are not
available to individual consumers. For example, tires
are very soarce, as well as automobile batteries. It
1s necessary for the various embassies or legations to
apply to the Japanese officials when these are needed
and an application of the Turkish Legation for automobile
tires involved a wait of between four to five months
before the permission and the tires were received.
Taxis are stated to be in very poor condition. They
all now consume charooal and the tires are not in
very good state.
Induetrial Development:
An important aspect of the Japanese picture that
was emphasized by the recent arrival was the fact that
industrial activity was on the inorease. Although he
had no conorete data with regard to the looations and
types of plants involved in this growing industrial
activity, certain observations that were made in this
regard are considered of value. For example, labor
is so scarce that it is practically impossible for
business firms to find office boys, and foreigners
cannot find house amahs for less than lo0 yen a month,
since this labor source is now being tapped by the
war faotories, whioh are said to pay between 4-5 yen
a day for unskilled labor.
This office's informant states that plants are
going up throughout Japan and many German engineers
and teehnicians are being busily employed in Japan to
this end. The principal types of industrial plants
mentioned
10177
1017

=== Page 126 === (confidence 0.99)
mentioned by the traveler as being upon the increase
were those manufacturing sulphate of ammonia and those
devoted to the German process of securing liquified
fuel from coal, which is said within recent months to
have been intensified in public and press discussions.
The Kawasaki river between Tokyo and Yokohama is
alleged to be one section witnessing this inoreased
industrialization, although the informant has heard,
without possessing any definite particulars, that
this industrialization process is more lively in the
Osaka area.
Commodity Prices and Rationing:
The new arrival reported that the Japanese autho-
rities have succeeded in stemming any possible inflation
within the country fairly successfully and that prices
are rigidly controlled, so that there is no undue hard-
ship from this aspect of the economic situation for the
Japanese public, outside of tne necessity for abiding
by the strict rationing procedure.
Upon the traveler's ueparture from Japan lo0 momme
(600 grams) of sugar were allocated to foreigners each
month. This office's source believed tnet for Japanese the
ration was at least 60 percent less. The official price
for 100 momme of sugar was about 25 sen, although on the
blaok market a like quantity of sugar could be secured
for yen 1.50 to yen 1.80. The bread ration for foreigners
was half a kin per person aaily, which is believed to be
slightly less than a pound. The flour ration, again for
foreigners, wes about 8 pounds a month. Each person was
entitled to only loo momme a month of meat and the offi-
oial price was said to be yen 1.60, elthough, in common
with other black market prices, comaodities could be
secured at prices approximately five to sir times greater
than the official figure.
After the war with the United States had begun,
a system of clothing ration ticket was instituted, which
was based on the principle of l00 points to be consumed
in a year. The informant stated that in February this
olothing rationing system was revised to comprise only
80 points in ration tiokets serving to cover an indivi-
dual's olothing requirements. Under this system 60
points are stated to be deduoted for a suit and a shirt
takes 12 olothing ration points. There is a olothing
black market, however, and the Consulate General's
informant states that he seoured three yards of wool
material, not or the best quality, at a oost of 250
yen. Fer better material the price allegedly amounts
to as muoh  400 to 500 yen.

The Curreney Black Market:
Some interesting comments upon the black market and
currency, effective upon the informant's departure from
Japan, were given. There is stated to be a black market
in American dollars in Japan, whereby one can secure an
American dollar for between yen 7.30 to yen 5, which was
the experience of a person well known to the traveler.
In Shanghai the blaok market is quoted as enabling 14
yen to buy an American doller, while in Peking in April
the rate was 15 yen to the dollar. In Shangai 70 C.R.B.
(Central Reserve Bank of China) notes for one American
dollar was the black market rate.
Travel Restrictions and Railway Traffic:
Travel for foreigners in Japan is now extremely
restricted and foreigners without official passports
are unable to travel from Tokyo to Yokohama. To go
there it is necessery for a foreigner to apply at the
police headquarters of tne district in whicn ne resiues,
where a form must be filled out iving all the parti-
culars of the trip. Foreign government officials are
said to be granted travel permits for local travel such
as between Tokyo and Yokohama on a tnree months basis,
while Germans and Italiens are the recipients of special
travel passes, which enable them to go from Tokyo to
Kobe without securing a permit in advance.
The railroads are stated to be exceedingly congested
and some passenger trains have been withdrawn, in order
to secure increased right of way for needed freight. This
oongestion is alleged to be much more marked than at the
outbreak of the war.
Travel from Japan:
When the Turkish diplomatic party made its plans
to leave Japan it enoountered no difficulties with the
Japanese in its departure plans. The group finally
left on April lst and proceeded by way of Shimonoseki,
thence by boat to Fusan and Korea, followed by a railway
journey to Manohuria and thence orossing into Siberia.
The trip from Tokyo to Shimonoseki was made without
escort and the trip from Shimonosek1 to Fusan was made
during the day. A life-boat drill was held immediately
after the departure from port, whioh drill was preceded
by a leeture given by the captain, in which he omphasized
the need for taking safety measures, of knowing how to
use the life preservers, and of being acquainted with
the life-boat stations.
giberian

Biberian Observations:
Upon crossing the border into Siberia, the party
as estorted by a Russian "tourist representative"
until they reached Tiflis. This person clearly was a
Government official and spoke both French and English,
with which the representative conversed with the party
during the trip.
Russian morale in the war was stated to be very
high, although the people clearly were suffering from
many privations. Propaganda was most intensive against
the Germans and anti-Nazi posters were widespread. In
all towns and railway stations where the party stopped
on route the news was broadcast through loud-speakers
at periodio intervals.
Indicative of feeling towards the Americans and
the British was an incident in Siberia described by
the traveler as having occured when he went to a Russian
bath-house. The traveler, inquiring in English for hot
water, soap and towels, was immediately given the best
faoilities, and, having some knowledge of Russian
although concealing this, he heard the Russians enthu-
siastically remarking that he must be an American or
British engineer who had come to assist Russia in the
war.

NDUM OF CONVERSATION
July 9, 1943
Subject: Signifioance of Extraordinary Session of
Japanese Diet at Tokyo, June 16-18, 1943.
Present:
General Wang Peng-sheng, the Generalissimo's
Expert on Japanese Affairs and Head of the
Institute of International Problems of the
Chinese Military Council.
Dr. T. T. Mar, General Wang's Assistant
Mr. Boehringer
General Wang stated that, in his opinion, the most
important work accomplished by the extraordinary Diet
session, which met at Tokyo from June 16 to 18, 1943
inclusive, was the passing of the industrial readjust-
ments bill. Mis comments on the signiricance of this
law were given under a number of headings, ineluding
finaneial, political, economic, military and social.
Before taking up his disoussion of the law under the
foregoing headings, General Wang stated that its general
objeotives were to shift all machinery possible to wartime
production, to sorap all obsolete and exoess maohinery in
order to obtain iron and other metals, and to place the
released skilled workers in munitions plants. Regarding
unskilled laborers, he expressed the opinion that certain
numbers would be absorbed by the armed foroes. He said
that, in accordanoe with the industrial readjustments law,
the Japanese Government hoped to inorease efficiency in
produotion, to exeroise still greater control in allocation
of skilled laborers and of resources, and to reduce inter-
ministry friotion and confusion to the minimum.
Following are the opinions expressed by General Wang
on the signifioant features of the industrial readjustments
law:
1. Finanoial: The Diet passed a budget amounting to
Yon 6,100,000,000. 0f this amount, at most Yen 1,500,000,000
appears to be set aside for industrial readjustments, but a
figure of perhaps Yen 1,000,000,000 might be more correot.
The remainder appears to be earmarked for military expendi-
tures, a point which was not made clear in Japanese broad-
casts. Funds required for exeoution or the industrial read-
justments law, designed largely to reimburse faotory owners
for maohinery taken up by the Government or sorapped and also
to make payments to small manufacturers and employees to
enable them to tide over the period of unemployment, will not
be paid out in cash but mainly in credit notes on the national
treasury. Only about ten per cent of the total amount required
will be paid out in cash, while the balance will be covered
by credit notes.
The implioations of the foregoing appear obvious. The
Japanese, desiring to keep secret all information regarding
expeaditures for military purposes, have passed the huge
budget of Yen 6,100,000,000 for the announoed purpose of
sarrying out the provisions of the industrial reedjustments
lew. Aesuelly, however, only from 16 to 24 per oent of the
total will be used for this purpose, the balanee apparently
being set asise for military purposes.
2.

=== Page 140 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
2. Political: The significance of the industrial
readjustments law from the political standpoint falls
under the following sub-headings: (a) The inference to
be made is that the law will cause the complete downfall
of politioal parties formerly backed by the plutocrats
and industrial magnates in Japan. Contrariwise, the power
of the Government is greatly increased and at the expense
of the plutoorats and magnates; (b) Medium-sized and small
manufacturers, formerly leagued with fascist and radioal
groups of rightist tendenoies, will be wiped out after the
kaw is fully enforoed. These rightist groups will be taken
over by the military and will assert themselves to an in-
creasing extent in the future; (c) The law will affeet those in the
highest strata of society in Japan, particularly the dosest
advisers of the Emperor formerly backed by financiers and
industrialists. Enforoement of the law will lessen their
influenee and consequently increase the influence of the
militarists. "Gentlemen close to the Emperor will be re-
plaoed by swashbucklers", he said.
The law is also of significance to the United Nations.
In planning our propaganda work for Japanese consumption,
it must be remembered that all liberal elements have now
been driven from power in Japan and are completely impotent.
We formerly believed we could support the liberal element,
but now we have no alternative than to fight it out with
the militarists. The war must be carried to Japan, the
milftarists crushed and the munitions plants levelled to
the ground.
3. Eoonomic: The economic significance of the indus-
trial readfustments law is of major importanoe. The passing
of this law should not be construed as pointing to early
collapsedr Japan. It should be considered as being a new
step designed to regenerate Japanese economio power. The
situation may be likened to that of a sick man who needs
blood transfusions from members of his family.
The gist of the projected readjustments, from the
eeonemic standpoint, is to shift all metals obtained by
serapping obsolete and unused maohinery to shipbuilding,
which is regarded as the most important item at the present
time. Ships are needed in order to bring resources from
the oceupied areas, for in this lies the only hope of sur-
vival of Japanese economy. The fact that the shipping

The 82nd Extraordinary Session of the Japanese
Diet and its Relation to Home and Foreign Affairs
In previous extraordinary sessions of the Japanese Diet
as recorded in history, convocations were made either for
the purpose of deolaration of war, such as in the 7th (against
China), the 2lst (agatnst Russia), the 34th (against Germany),
and the 77th (against Britain and Americal, or in connection
with re-election or home affairs, especially concerning fin-
anoial matters as in the 3rd, 6th, 12th, 18th, 32nd, 39th,
46th, 53rd, 55th, 58th and 61st. The extraordinary sessions
convened in conneotion with war were not to deoide whether
war should be deolared or peace preserved, but only to
announce to the Diet the secret decision of the Emperor-
in-Counoil, to ask the Diet to pass the supplementary budget
bill, to express the attitude adopted by the Government towards
its people and foreign countries, and to charge the.people
with the responsibility of prosecution of war. Actually, the
Diet has no power to vote for or against the deolaration of
war, as the oonstitution defines that the power of deolaration
of war or negotiation for peace rests entirely with the
Emperor. Hence it is not exceptional if the 82nd session is
being held solely for the purpose of declaring the Government's
polioy in foreign matters. As the parties in the present Diet
are not as complete as before, it cannot serve as many purposes
as it did in other extraordinary sessions. In short, the true
aspoot of Japan's polioy in foreign matters, and the opportune
time fixed for suoh adoption will not have been ohanged in any
way as a result of holding this session, for the whole matter
deponds upon the secret decision of the Emperor-in-Council.
The Domei News Agenoy in its report of May 28 reported
that the agenda of the session would center around the question
of readjustment of industries, aiming perticularly at produc-
tion. The wholesale readjustment of the industries, it
reported, did not only mean to re-arrange the functional
capacity for production, but also to reform indirectly the
nature of economio production. This may be regarded as an
"Industrial Revolution", and is what Domei termed "The Radical
Changes" or "Rovolutionization of Industries". But the fact
is that Japanese Imperialism has been pushed one step further
towards that of state-ownership. The following changes may
take place:
1. Modirioation of management, ohanging private management
to state management or state control.
2. Suspension of manufacture of all civilian requirements
and some military requirements in favor of productive power
for turning out military requirements most urgently needed.
Lately, Japan has been alarmed by the produotive power of
Ameriea. Knowing that they lag behind in the produotion of
airerart and shipping, the Japanese are now putting forward
the most rigid demands which will surpass any country in con-
trol, comeentration of productive power, and saorifice. An
artlele in the "Chuo Koran" of February reads, inter alia,
since

viowpoint on the appraisal of America's productive power had
been turned into that of pre-war days of the Pacifio". The
incitement, and the great fear the Japanese have can in this
way be olearly peroeived.
The two possible changes mentioned in the above are
naturally unacceptable to the financiers, as the strong

=== Page 157 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No.1 to Despatch Polish Series No.397
dated September 9, 1943
SECRET:
10th August 1943.
Disorientation in Japanese policy.
A Japanese diplomat in a conversation which took place
in a neutral country on the 9th August made the following
analysis of the international situation and of its consequences
for Japan:
1. Mussolini's downfall has caused a far greater effect
in Germany than in Italy, Rumania or Hungary. This becomes
apparent when observing German internal conditions. At the
same time no deterioration has been noticed in the morale of
German troops on the Eastern front.
2. In view of the above a separate peace between Germany
and Russia would not necessarily bring about a strengthening of
Germany's military potentiality to be directed against the Anglo-
Americans, but could rather cause a further deepening of
depression inside Germany. If Germany makes peace with Russia
by agreeing to accept Russian peace conditions, every German
will take it as a disaster, and no propaganda will be able to
convince him to the contrary. Only an alliance with Russia
could therefore enable Germany to launch an attack on the
Western front, but even then the results would be doubtful. The
Russian territorial conditions, even if accepted by Germany,
could not be fulfilled until the war in the West is over, and
to this the Russians would hardly agree. Germany, however, must
keep the territory of the Ukraine until the end of the war,
because otherwise she would be threatened with a catastrophical
shortage of food.
3. Even if peace between Russia and Germany is concluded
the danger always remains that Germany will not be able to
survive such a political shock. On the other hand if Russia
accepts the assumption that peace is indivisible, she may
demand from Germany a capitulation on both fronts, i.e. also on
the Western front, thus bringing about a speedy termination of
the war. In such an event Japan would be left entirely alone.
4. In view of the above it would seem to be the best for
Japan to contribute to a neutralization of Russia by threatening
her in the Far East. (This eventuality has arisen in connection
with the observed lowering of morale inside Germany). Such a
move would undoubtedly bring considerable relief to the German
Army, whose situation on the Eastern front is getting more and
more difficult. Thus Japan is being faced with the problem of
declaring war on Russia. But in this connection first of all
the teohnical possibilities must be taken into consideration.
Japan could only declare war on Russia if she were sure that
she would be able within a fortnight to liquidate the Soviet
air bases in the region of Vladivostock-Khabarovsk, and then in
the second phase to launch a successful attack in the West, thus
throwing the Russian foroes behind Chyta and the Baikal. Only
under such circumstances Japan could risk a war with Russia, for
if she could not liquidate Russia at once, she would expose
herself to the dangers of Russian and American bombings.
5. Japan mast withdraw from the many fents on which she now
fights and which absorb too much of ber streagth. First of all
abo/

=== Page 158 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
she must end the war with China. Japan now fights against
Csang-Kai-Shek whose forces are composed of two groups. One is
Csang-Kai-Shek's Army which does not represent great military
value. The Chinese resistance is concentrated in the Communist
Army which is well armed and equipped, and commanded by the most
able general and politican of today's China, in one word a man
who in every respect surpasses Czang-Kai-Shek. The latter is
entirely dependent on the support of the Communist Army. Japan
ought therefore to come to an understanding with the C. -in- C.
of the Communist Army and then the war in China will come to an
end automatically. An obstacle for this as well as for the
reaching of an understanding with Russia has been the Anti-
Komintern Pact.
The recent developments point out that Japan
ought to abandon the Anti-Komintern Pact, which is all the more
easy as formally the Komintern was disbanded and as a political
organization it does not exist any more. When Japan takes such
a step only then appropriate conditions will be created for
conversations with the Chinese Communist Army and also Russia.
But before this is effected changes must be carried out in Japan's
political life; all the generals who are responsible for the
present policy of the country must go, and amongst them Oshima
and many others.
The above argumentation of the Japanese diplomat shows that
in the Japanese political circles new political trends have become
apparent. It seems that the Japanese have realized that it is not
easy to bring about an understanding between Russia and Germany;
they are also afraid that if such an understanding is reached it
may have the effect of weakening Germany politically and hasten
the fall of the Third Reich, in which event Japan will be left
alone. There is a second alternative : to help Germany directly
by attacking Russia, but when taking into consideration such a
solution fears arise whether Japan will have enough strength to
beat Russia.
Consequently the alternatives for Japan to follow can be
summed up, as follows:
To abandon the Anti-Komintern Pact and the present Axis
polioy; this would mean a total break down of the Berlin-Rome-
Tokio Axis.
To change the present Japanese policy by dismissing the
present military team of politicians.
To endeavour to settle the conflict with China by Russian
intermediary.
To deepen the relations with Russia.

with the Japanese Emperor in the post-war period, was
evidently occasioned by Mr. Grew's recent speech in the
United States, and may for that reason be of special in-
6
terest. The writer does not take a very definite stand on
5
the question but puts both sides of the argument for the

in history for dealing with vanquished deities." The writ-

considered, "appear skeptical about preserving the status
quo, although no official statements have been made."

Province, in regard to conditions in Japan prior to July 1,
94.00

894.00/11
Summary: According to a Chinese businessman who lived
in Nagasaki for 22 years prior to his departure therefrom
on July 1, 1943, food and clothing rationing in the Nagasaki
area is rigidly enforced. The metals snortage is so acute
that each family is allowed only one metal cooking pot or
pan. The number of ships regularly plying between Nagasaki
and Shanghai was reduced as of July 1, 1943, by sinkings,
6
from a total of four ships to but one and this was subsequent-
9
ly reported to have been sunk. Morale of the common people
is reported as being good. According to other Chinese inter-
viewed by Mr. Calawell, conditions at Shanghai were said to
be worse than those in Japan. The Japanese at Shanghai re-
portedly were dismantling a large scuttled Italian ship,
possibly the Conte Verde, in order to salvage the metal.
Respectfully yours,

ORT ON CONVERSATIONS NEAR FUTSING, FUKIEN PROVINCE,
TTH CHINESE RECENTLY RETURNED FROM JAPAN AND SHANGHAI
(Roport by Mr. John Caldwell, OWI representative at Foochow)
December 2, 1943
On November 29 and 30 I visited several villagos on
the Lungtien Peninsula southeast of Futsing where I mot and
talked with several persons who had recently returned from
Japan and Shanghai. The best informant was a man of 55,
named Kiang, who had been in the cloth retailing business
in Nagasaki for 22 years. He left there July 1, 1943 on the
Shanghai Maru. He was in Shanghai several months awaiting
transportation, then came south by way of Shen Chia Men,
arriving in October. The substance of his report follows:
FOOD RATIONING: The rice ration is nine ounces per day,
rigidly enforced for both rich and poor. One half ounce
of mien per month.
Sweet potatoes - 3 catties per family per day.
Irish potatoes - lt catties per family per day.
Vegotables - one hoad per family per day (daikon or cabbago?-Ed.).
Cooking oil - 12 ounces per month per five people.
Soap - one yen per month per person.
CLOTHING RATIONING: Point system rigidly in force, with each
person allowed lo0 points per year. An ordinary men's suit
with two pieces requires 60 points. Shoes, socks, thread,
etc. all must come from same lo0 points. All cloth is made
from grass now (probably vegetable fibre-Ed.).
METALs: Each family is allowed but one metal cooking pot or
pan. It may not be replaced if broken. Iron, copper and all
other types of metals taken up by the Government. within re-
cont months temples have been robbed of their metals. Bridge
railings, etc. have been taken up. Most things are now made
of wood, even bicycles. But recently wood and lumber have
been hard to get. No new house construction is possible.
(Chinose Communiats report that most bronze and iron momorial
statues have been "recruited" for war-Ed.)
SHIPPING: Informant said that shipping situation in Nagasaki
is acute. Originally 4 ships plied between Nagasaki and Shang-
hai - Nagasaki, Shanghai, Osaka and Tokyo Marus. All but the
Shanghai Maru had been sunk by July. (Roport of Shanghai Maru
sinking bolow.)
GENERAL: All young poople are gone. Girls from 16 years up
work in war plants. At harvest time city people are assigned
to the farms to help harvest crops. The Government takes all
farm produce at fixed prices. Morale of the common people
is reperted as good. In order to raise money the Japanese
Government has made insurance compulsory for all peeple. Each
citisem buys $2, 000 (probably yen) worth of insurance for which
he pays $24 per year. I gathered this was a form of enforeed
Aino-mura (
Talked wath

=== Page 175 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
Talked with two other men recently returned from Shanghai-
one within two days. Both came via Shen Chia Men. One had
been in Japan until recently. He reported living cenditions
An Shanghai worse than those in Japan, with the Japanese re-
moving all goods from Shanghai. Japanese reported to be tear-
ing up large scuttled Italian ship (Conte Vorde?) to salvage
the metal. They reported the ships listed above as sunk, also
reported that the Shanghai Maru was sunk by a submarine two
weeks. ago. One of the few survivors was a man with whom my
informant had talked. Only small ships plying between Shang-
hai and Japan now, and run infrequently. They estimated Ja-
panese strength in Shanghai area now at twelve to fifteen
thousand.

Japanese morale. The morale of the Japanese popula-
tion is stil1 high but the élan and enthusiamm of l942
have disappeared with the "stalemating" of the war and a
defense paychology now pervades the nation. There is
some war weariness, especially among the businessmen, but
no apparent national brooding over the future or defeatim
or complaining. The nation has a good general idea of the
progress of the war but does not talk sbout it and seems
calm and not frightened. The people are "fully prepared"
JAN 22 ?!" walks around the oity blooks where there are sidewalks
and shelters in all back yards and open lots. In the air
raid drills the people now take their bedding quilts with
them to the alit trenohes and hold them over their heads
for protection. The. whole Empire is said to go on the
alert whenever it is attacked at any one point, say
Paramushiro or Marous Island.
Commodity situation. The economio situation of the
whele Far East is unamimously and emphatically deelared to
be growing pregressively worse. Cemmodities, especially
arban pepulation is concerned. In Japanese cities in

=== Page 178 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
Tubereulosis is on the inorease in Japanese cities and
resistance to disease is declining -- colds are said to
linger om indefinitely. The soldiers and sailors, however,
are well fed and healthy. Because transpertation for
meving produce and catehes of fish to the cities is
inadequate, the rural and fishing populations consume a
proportionately larger portion of their produce and on the
whole are gotting enough to eat. In Chinese cities, the
food situation is somewhat better than in Japan beeause
of black markets and ineffeotive controls, but more and
more substitutes of inferior types are being used... The
annual textile allowance per person is absurdly small. One of
the articles most difficult to obtain, even when one possesses
a ration ticket for it, is sewing and monding thread.
Causes of Shortages. Various explanations are given
for t he aoute shortage of food and every other necessity
of life. Some explanations are positive, others speculative.
In Japan Proper the declining number of powered vehieles
and craft to haul produce and fish from producing areas to
urban centers and of ships to move rice, grain and beans
from the Asiatic mainland is generally cited as the chief
cause of the food shortage. It is a fact that trucks and
automobiles have become very scaroe and that most of them
burn charcoal gas instead of gasoline. It is a fact alse
empty and lifeless, an occasional convoy or unescorted
wooden ship being the only visitors. And yet food is being
raised as before it is admitted. What is happening to it?
Where is the former surplus of millions of tons of soybeans,
grain and rice from Manchuria and Korea going to? Most
guesses are that food is being stock-piled by the
Government -- and it is true that rationed rice and grains
are often wormy, suggesting the use of ad stooks. However,
it does not seem logioal that the Japanese Government is
hearding food supplies to the extent of undermining the
health and strength of its great urban population. Very
probably insuperable difficulties in distribution are
doveloping.
Wooden ships. The wooden ship construotion program is
not as sucoessful as Japanese propaganda has led us to
believe. The ships being turned out under this pregram,
200 or 300 tons burden, seem seareely to have caught the
eye of the repatriates or to be accomplishing much in
actuality.
War industries

=== Page 179 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
ar industries. It is generally believed that thee
indastries are being kept up and e ven expanded. Otherwise
it is asked, where are all the coal and metals and chemieal
that are apparently being produced in inereasing quantities
but being denied to oivilians going to? Dwellings in
both the Empire and ocoupied China are being stripped of
doorkmobs, iron railings and metal pots and pans, presumably
for munitions production, as they do not return to the
eivilian population in any form.
Occupied China. Literally millions of Japanese have
swarmed over to ocoupied China since the begimning of the
Paeifie War and are engaged chiefly in acquiring and
controlling the mineral, crop, livestook, hide, wool,
tobacco and cotton output in the locality. Japanese controls
and preemptions of this output are causing mounting dis-
content and soarcities. Japanese attempts& conciliation
are ineffective. Chinese contempt for Japanese in their
midst and obstructionism of Japanese activities are growing.
Pacification is not making much headway. If anything,
guerrilla activity is regaining vitality. In general the
whole situation in occupied China is becoming more confused
and heading toward chaos. Development of heavy industries
in Manchuria and North China seems to be continuing, whereas
in central and southern China the Japanese appear to be
interested only in stripping the land of its capital
•quipment and ourrent produotion.

I have the honor to transmit copy of a special
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
article, "Japan from the Inside" by Gunther Stein,
Chungking, published in The Manchester Guardian of

SION OF
29 1944
Mr. Stein, whose comment is based upon impressions
obtained from a Chinese escaped from Japan, says that
the attitude of the Japanese people toward the war is
now completely fatalistic. He concludes that early
Allied bombings will have a surprisingly drastic effect
on the overstrained people and that raids will not
stimulate hatred of the enemies but of the Japanese
Government.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
February 19, 1944
With Chungking's underlying despatch
no. 2109 of February 2, 1944 there 1s
attached a copy of a despatch from Mr. Drum-
right on detail at Sian enclosing a copy
of his memorandum of conversation with a
Chinese student who left Tokyo on October 26,
1943. In regard to the informant, Mr. Drum-
right states "Mr. Chiao, in my opinion, 1s
one of the most intelligent and sound Chi-
nese observers of Japanese conditions I
have had the opportunity of meeting, not-
withstanding his youth."
This memorandum contains one of the most
interesting and useful accounts of conaitions
in Japan during 1943 which have come to JA's
attention. It is recommended that it be read
by all officers of FE.
The memorandum is adequately summarized
in Mr. Drumright's covering despatch. In
addition, for those who do not have time to
read the complete memorandum, the paragraph
on page 1 entitled "Attitude of people in
Japan" is recommended. The final two para-
graphe on page 4 may also be of interest.
JA:Behninghoff:MLS

Enclosure No. l to Despatch
No. 2109, February 2, 1944,
American Embassy, Chungking.
Second Secretary on detail at Sian,
January 24, 1944.
No. 11
SubJeot: CONDITIONS IN JAPAN
The Honorable
C. E. Gauss,
Amerioan Ambassador,
Chungking.
Sir:
I heve the honor to enclose, as of probable interest
to the Embassy, a memorandum prepared on the basis of a
conversation had on January 22, 1944, with a Chinese
student recently arrived from Japan. I believe that my
informant is reasonably well informed on Japanese conditions
and developments and that his comments thereon, as I have
recorded them, are worthy of interest.
Summary of memorandum. The enlightened section of the
Japanese populace 1s pessimistic over the future of the war.
The unmalightened section, fed by the propaganda of the
Japanese militarists, is still confident of victory. Fear
of the might of Russia and the United States is growing.
Repression of the liberties of the Japanese people is
stringent. Induotion of students into military service
started in September l943. Civilians were removed from
strategio coastal areas in July or August 1943. Civilians
are dispersing from populated centers in anticipation of
air raids. Rationing of food and clothing is general.
Many essential articles are unobtainable. The populace is
generally underfed. There is an acute shortage of manpower,
made up in part by the general employment of women in all
kinds of work. Occidentals vere seen working on the docks
at Niigata. Colonists are still goins to Manohuria.
Respectfully yours,
Everett F. Drumright
Second Secretary of Embassy
on detail at Sian

MEMORANDUM
SubJeot: CONDITIONS IN JAPAN
I had a long conversation today with a Mr. Chiao
Wen-sheng (who because he has a family residing in Peiping
whe has spent the past two years in a Tokyo preparatory
engimeering college. He left Tokyo October 26, l943. Mr.
Chiao said that he had kept a comprehensive diary while in
turning to China. Mr. Chiao, in my opinion, is one of the
most intelligent and sound Chinese observers of Japanese
conditions I have had the opportunity of meeting, notwith-
standing his youth.
Attitude of people in Japan. The people of J pan (
according to Mr. Chiao) must be divided into two general
classfrioations for purposes of identifying their attitude:
(1 those with eduoation and enlightenment who are to be
found in the cities for the most part; ind (2 the unlettered
or rathor unlightened who are for the most part to be found
residing in thecountry districts. There is a general reali-
zation among the intelligentsia end persons with perception
that Japan is doomed to ultimate defeat. Their attitude is
one of deep gleom and foreboding for the future. This
attitude is general among educators, merchants, professional
people, et cetera. In the country among the lower classes.
however, the prevailing spirit is one of optimism. Here the
people are intoxicated with the propeganda of the Hapanese war
machine. They expeot a long but victorious war. They are not
yet conscious of the trend towerd defeat. With the defeat of
Italy, the gloom and pessimism among the middle and upper
olasses in Japan has deepened perceptibly. But even though
defeat is regarded as inevitable among people of peroeption,
there is no evidenee at this time of a trend toward surrender
or capitulation. The spirit is rather one of grim sacrifice,
to go forward to death, sadly it is true, yet tdthigrim resolve
and detormination to die for Emperor end country. At the
countless receptions held for those joining the colors the
general atmosphere is one of sedness mingled with tears and
there is muoh talk of going to the front to meet death.
Growing fear of Russia. The fear among the Jepanese of
Russia is deep and growing (according to Mr. Chiao).
United States regarded as strong foe. The Japanese
people are coming to realize that they have met a strong and
redoubtable foe in the United States (according to Mr. Chiao).
Fear of the enormous production records of the United States
prevails and is used by the Japanese militarists as a club
to foster inereasing production in Japan. Gone is the
feeling that the people of the United States were too soft
and luxury-loving and peace-loving to make the effort required
to defeat the Axis Powers. Intelligent Japanese now easily
pereeive that the Japanese militarists miscalouleted in regard
to the mmrtial spirit and energy of the United States.
Britain regarded as secondary foe. Great Britain's
military power 1s regarded by Japanese as being secondary to
that ef the United States and Russia (according to Mr. Chiao),
pessibly beeause British pewer is so far removed from the
theatre of the Paeifie confliot.
messien ef Japeneze moale. The Japanese people are
Then

=== Page 194 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
re is no freedom of speech and control of the press and
ublieations is most rigid. The people are reluctant to
diseuss the war in public. Plainclothesmen are to be found
everywhere. The Japanese radio is almost wholly devoted to
the dismomination of propagande, and the trend of the press
im inereasingly in this direction. It is the purpose or this
in the announeement of war communiques and diseussions. The
poople, of Japan have no way of knowing precisely about war
devolopments.
Induetion of students. Japanese middle school and
colloge students were not1ried September 15, l943 that those
over 21 must report for military service. Those studying
soienee and allied subjects were exempted. At the same time
announoemont was made that Japanese males between the ages
of 17(?) and 45 must report for consoription, whether for
induotion into military service or for labor. Those working
in eertain defined unessential occupations were notified that
they eould no longer serve in such occupations. These ineluded
barbers, bath house attendents, et cetera. Students reported
for physical examinations on September 25, 1943, and by the
latter part of October 1943 middle schools were partially
deserted and colleges even more vacant. Many sohools were
ferced to elose and in others edmeation was seriously hampered.
Many students left the colleges reluctantly. Care had to be
taken to prevent prospeotive induotees from various walks of
life from fleeing with a view to avoiding military life.
Removal of civilians from coastal areas. In July or August
1943 all civilians living in proximity to the obest of Japan in
the vicinity of military zones were required to remove therefrom.
Dispersal of civilians from populated centers. There is
fear In Japan of enemy eir attacks. It 1s prevalent among the
people, many of whom are dispersing to the country.
Economie conditions in Japan. Rationing is general, both
for food and elething. Clothing is rationed on a point system
of l00 points per year, of which an overcoat takes 63 points,
a pair of sox 3 points et cetera. Cotton cloth is unprocurable.
Wool is not available. The bilk of the Japanese people now wear
olothing made of rayon or silk. The Japanese people are becoming
shabby in appearance. The rayon clothing is not durable. There
is no leather for shoes which are now made of fish skin. The
steres are now depleted of stocks of goods. Several of the
large Tekyo department stores have had to close all but one or
twe floors because of dwindling supplies of goods. There is
very little produetion of consumer goods in Japan teday, the
faetories having been converted to the produetlon of war
materials. Most shops are selling stocks previously held.
There is no rubber for the Japanese people and also ne gasoline.
All shipping is devoted to the transport of war supplies and
persensel. The Japanese people are reseiving no material
benefit frem the seizure or the resourees of the south seas.
The Jepanese people are seriously underfed because the food
obtaimed by may of ratieaing is insuffieient and poor in quality.

rem January or February 1943 those Japanese fortunate enough
obtain rice had to start eating the unpolished variety. The
use of polished rice was forbidden from that time. Trawel by
plane in Japan is reserved solely for official use. There is
no longer coal in Japan for heating purposes. Schools, shops,
publie offiees and the like are no longer heated. There is a
great seareity.of metals of all kinds. All sorap metal has
Iong sinee been gathered for military use. There is an acute
shortage of paper. Books are extremely scarce. There have been
no new books printed, that is, text books, sinoe 1938. The
railmays are not exceptionally crowded and continue to run on
time; the same applies to the electric tram service which
funetions with its old time efficiency. Wood is increasingly
used in place of metal.
Foreigners working on docks. Mr. Chiao stated that when
he went to embark on the Hakmsan Maru(@/i/乙) at Niigata, on
the west coast of Japan, on November 2, 1943, to return to
China, he saw working on the docks about 200 or more foreigners.
He said that these foreigners were wearing trousers of a deep
blue color, worn brown leather shoes, old felt hats. Each
foreigner had a number on the outer clothing on his back.
These foreigners, that is, Occidentals, appeared to range
between 20 and 40 years of age, according to Mr. Chiao. They
were performing ordinary coolie dook work. Mr. Chiao said
that when he witnessed them they were unloading iron ore from
flat cars and placing it into warehouses. They were also
pushing flatoars on which the iron ore had been transported.
Mr. Chiao said that he saw e Japanese with e long heavy club
in his hand supervising the foreigners in their work. This
Japanese issued his commands in Japanese to the foreigners.
Mr. Chiao said that he did not see the foreigners talking
among themselves at any time vhen under his observation. He
said he could not ascertain their nationality. He said that
they looked tired and thin, and that they were working in the
rain. He stated that the Japanese overseer wore a rubber rain-
coat while the foreigners wore nothing more than a fiber
matting over their shoulders (common among coolies in China
and Japam) to proteot them from the rain.
Shortage of manpower in Japan: employment of women. Mr.
Chiao Said that Japan is suffering from an acute manpower
shortage. He said that this is reflected espeeially in
deereased agrioultural production and the chronie shortage
of foodsturfs. The number of women working in the faetories
and shops, on communieations, and on the ferms has reached a
new high. He reported that 60 per cent of the railway workers
are new eomprised of women. They now pull the few rlekshaws
left in Japan. Mr. Chiao said that apart from the foreigners
emgleyed in doek work at Niigata virtually all other doek
workbrs were women.
Trim from Japan to China Mr. Chiao said that he had to
aly Severel times to the Tokye poliee before they would
asmit kim to leave Tekye. They finally gave him a pass when

 no guns. In the harbor there were a great mmny

Mr. Chiae said that during the journey across to Rashin,
Koree, everyone had his lifebelt besided him at all times.
Mr. Chiao maid that the voyage from Niigata to Rashin took
53 hours. As their ship arrived in port at 6 a.m. of November
5, 1943, a small vessel about the size of the Hakusan Maru
was seen leaving Rashin. l'here were no other vessels in port
at that time. There were numerous tugs and barges in the
harber, however. Mr. Chiao said that once ashore in Rashin
he observed a great number of what appeared to be new ware-
houses all of which were camouflaged. He said that he saw
several factory chimneys at Rashin. Mr. Chiao remarked that
the Chinese population in Rashin has dwindled to about 300
persons. From Rashin Mr. Chiao traveled by railway through
Changehun and Shanhaikuan to Peiping. He reported that the
railway is now double-tracked along the entire route. He
reported that the exit of people at  hanhaikuan is olosely
controlled and that none may go south unless in possession
of-passes. Mr. Chiao remarked that the examination of
Japanese at Rashin and Shanhaikuan was partioularly stringent
owing to the penchant of Japanese to endeevor to avoid
military service.
General view of conditions in Japan. Mr. Chiao expressed
the viow that Japanese internal conditions are such that Japan
will probably collapse within another year. He summed up the
chler problems confronting the Japanese as (1 shortage of
food and daily commodities, and (2 shortage of manpower. He
said that the Japanese had controlled commodity prioes with
remmrkable sueeess, so much so, in fact, that there were no
longer eemmodities on the markets.
Mr. Chiao expressed the view that the Japanese people
are not yet ready to eapitulate. But he said that a few
deeisive Allied victories over the Japanese Army will probably
result in the fall of the Tojo cabinet and a revision of
attitwee. Mr, Chiao remarked in this general conneetiem that
it is commen kmowledge in Japan that an underground struggle
is eonstantly going on between the politioians and the
milfteriste on the one hand, and the Army and Navy on the
otmer. In short, it is Mr. Chiao's view that conditions in
ganeral in Japan are far worse than most outsiders seem te
regard them as being.

Economic conditions in Japan. rationing is general, both
for food and clothing. Clothing is rationed on a point system
of lo0 points per year, of which an overcoat takes 63 points.
a pair of sox 3 points et cetera. Cotton cloth is unprocurable.
Wool 1s not available. The bilk of the Japanese people now wear
olothing mede of reyon or silk. The Jepanese people are becoming

very little production of consumer roods in Japen today, the
factories havin: been oonverted to the produotion of war
materials. Most shops are selling stocks previously held.
There is no rubber for the Japanese people and also no gasoline.
All shipping is devoted to the transport of wer supplies and
personnel. The Japanese people are receiving no material
benefit from the seizure of the resouroes of the south seas.
The Japanese people are seriously underfed because the food

Mr. Moffat raised the question as to why the Proposed
Political Directive as drafted applied only to the Caroline
Islands when the Economic Directive previously adopted applies
to all of the Japanese Mandated Islands in the Central Pao-
ific. After a general discussion the following motion made
by Mr. Moffat was unanimously carried.
"That the title of the Proposed Political Directive be
changed to read 'Political Directive for the Military Gov-
ernment of the Japanese Landated Islands in the Central
Pacific' and that this Directive supersede the previously
adopted 'Political Directive for the Military Government
of the Marshall Islands' dated 27 December 1943."
3. Each article of the Directive was read aloud by
Lieut. Votaw, and separately considered and discussed by
the committee. Thereafter the Committee agreed on the var-
1ous articles of the Directive as follows:
Art. 1. Accepted as drafted.
Art. 2. It was moved by Mr. Moffat and unanimously
carried: "that the letter 's' be added to the word 'area'
in the first sentence of article 2.
It was moved by Mr. Johansen and unanimously carried:
"that the phrase 'in accordance with international law'
be added to the last sentence of article 2.
Art. 3. It was moved by Mr. Johansen and unanimously
carried: "that the phrase 'or with the interests of the
United States' be deleted from article 3.

Moscow newspapers of February 25 publish two
Tokyo despatches dealings with internal conditions in
Japan:
One of these despatches devotes five column inches
to citing and quoting ASAHI and TOKYO SHIMBUN regarding
unsatisfactory progress being made in decentralizing
the enterprises and dispersing the population of
Japanese cities. ASAHI since September the Government
has considered this an important measure of national
policy. The newspaper states that the decentralisati
must be carried out without delay and that the presents
tempo is very unsatisfactory in view of the stage now
PS/CF
reached by the war. In this work many unresolved
diffioulties are still observed.
TOKYO

=== Page 213 === (confidence 0.97)
Pebruary 20, 8 a.m., from Mosoow
TOAYO SHINBU observes that the tempo of reducing
population of cities cannot be considered satiafactors
"at the present time when the ememy has reathed the
Mershall Islands. le speak thus because we fear that
something unusual may happen to Tokyo. The Government
must direct what should be done. If enterprises, shops
and compenies can be dispersed that will reduce street
traffic appreciably".
The other despatch cites Domei to the effect that
beginning February 16 the Japanese Government parallel
with the increase of direct taxes in Japan and Korea
increased four indirect texes including those on
necessities, on recreational establishments on theaters
and a special tax.
These items were published just under London
despatches reporting Allied communique on Pacific opera-
tions and President's statement concerning sinking of
over l000 Japanese transports and barges by United States
foroes during 1943. A total of almost 14 colum inches
of space was devoted to the Pacifio war and Japan, which
is most extensive coverage given to this arem in the
daizy press in news items in over a year with the exoep-

1MORANDUM
Subject: CONDITIONS IN JAPAN.
The following information in regard to conditions in
Japan was obtained indirectly from a student who left Japan
in September 1943 to return to China.
Morale of Japanese people. During the period of
prolonged Japanese victories in the early part of the war
the Japanese people were naturally happy nd confident of
the prowess of their righting foree.  However, following
the Japa ese def t at Attu nd tn ir withdrawal from
Kiska, their losses in the solomons nd in New Guinea,
the death of Yamomoto, et cetert, the J  unese Iublic
began to have its doubts of ultirte victory nd became
more and more disturbed. Indicutive of the public frame
of mind was  speoch delivered by former Aabassador Nomura
in which he was guoted as s ying tht the defense of the
Solomons was egual in importance to the defense of North
Africa and that what haprened in the olomons night decide
the course of the war in the Facific. although the Japanese
people have beon disturbed by recent military dovelopments,
in general they accept the Japanese Government's propaganda
as gospel truth and they nccorcingly entertain reat hopes
for the future. Generally speakine, the Japanese people
were supporting the Government whole-heartedly at the time
that informant left Japun. Hate of the people of Japan
for the United states rose perceptibly following the
Japanese defeat at Attu and the withdrawal from Kiska.
All Japanese feel the hardships of war. This is more
especially true of the intellectual classes who may be
said to be more antipathetic toward the war than any other
segment of the Japanese population. The shortage of consumer
goods is markedly felt by the Japanese populace. But the
power of the military class is so strong that it would be
very difficult for the people to manifest disapproval or
to oppose the prosecution of the war in any way. However,
because the war is apparently turning into a prolonged one,
the Government is said to fear that the people may become
subject to "fifth column" propaganda with disastrous results
to the prosecution of the war. Therefore, large numbers of
"political police" are utilized to spy on the people. An
important function of these police, who are everywhere,
is to examine into the people's thoughts.
Air raid precautions. Following the heavy allied
bombings of Europe during the past yoar, the people of
Japan have lived in very great fear of enemy air raids.
Mass terror is most noticeable in the large centers of
Tokyo, Yokohama, Kobe, Osaka, Nagoya and Kyoto. Nervous-
ness is everywhere evident in Japan. Following the
American aerial raid of april 18, 1942, the confidence of
the Japanese people in the Japanese air force waned con-
siderably and fear of further raids was very apparent.
Flaborate air raid precautions have been carried out in
Japan. A Young Women's Corps has been organized throughout
the country and the members have been trained to perform
air raid prevention activities. Before each house in the
cities of Japan will be found two small bags of sand, a
pa11

=== Page 218 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
pail of water (soya pail), one shovel, and one square
mat. Each person always has a gas mask of cotton ready
for instant donning, and a steel helmet (this presumably
has reference to persons engaged in air raid preventive
activities), and each such person has a special uniform
for such work. Many Japanese now wear these uniforms
almost daily. Practice air raid alerts are carried out
at least once each month. When enemy planes are sighted
whistles are blown for a minute and a flag, half white
incendiary bombs is said to be particularly great in
Japan. Therefore in addition to the sand and water placed
in front of each house large casks holding water are
placed at intervals  long the strects; and in many places
cisterns 20 feet deep and 16 feet in diameter have been
constructed to hold water for use in fighting fire.
Many water pumps are also supplied along the streets.
Curiously, the running water supply is said to be cut off
on the sounding of an aerial alert in Japanese cities.
anti-aircraft guns are said to be inst lled chiefly in
the towers of the higher buildings and in the parks of
Japanese cities. ir raid prevention ctivities are
reported to have been much intensified since the heavy
bombings of Germany.
Notes on Japanese aviation. Japanese air cadets are
enrolled between the  ges of 15 and 18. after threc years
of training they are sent to the front for combat activity.
General Doihara is now Superintendent General of Aviation
in Japan. Japanese planes operate in the following
formations:

three planes

nine planes

twenty-seven planes

I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatoh
no. 2167, February 16, 1944, in regard to conditions in
Japan, and to enclose copies of articles issued on
February 15 and 28, 1944, by the (Party) Ministry of
Information, Chungking, containing further information
on that subjeet as described by Chinese who left Japan
in October 1943.
ORIO
Summary. Food and clothing rationing beeame in-
creasingly severe and many foods such as beef, pork and
fish were available only in very small amounts. Bean
curd was no longer available, as all supplies were being
used for industrial purposes. Students dug slit tremehes
in cities for protection from air raids and alse worked
on farms. During the few months preeeding Oetober 1945
the Japanese became more and more gloomy and many people
1204
including students and professors, were arrested 
"dangerous thoughts" charges. A serious blow to Japanese
morale occurred with the death of Admiral Yamamote,
regarded in Japan as another Admiral Togo. At ieast two
thirds of Tokyo's inhabitaats were females. Many Japanese
went barefoot in rainy weather in order to preserte their
shoes. House to house census taking beeame more frequems,
mainly in connection with conseription and masion coupems.
COMMUNICATH
Even men above the age of 58 were called to sarve in the
citizens' militia corps. In order to get eneugh to eat
M
at Tokye restaurants, people mad to eat at three es mese
restaurants consecutively. The railways were reserved.
largely for troop transportatiom amd restrietions were
1944
rigidly enforced for travel by civilians. End of Sumary.

China Information Committee
Daily Bulletin -- No. 882
February 15, 1944
RETURN FROM TOKYO
"After I arrived in Chungking I went to a small
restaurant and ate a large serving of beef. I had not
eaten so much beef in Tokyo in all of the past two years."
This was said by Ying Tso, a Chinese student who was
graduated from the Tokyo Imperial University last summer
and recently arrived in Chungking. He was sent to Japan
(which he left in October l943) to study by the puppet
government in the Northeastern Provinces. After graduation
a brother arranged for him to work in a puppet bank in
Peiping. He was anxious to get away from Jap domination
and succeeded in making his way from Peiping to Chungking.
He said that Japan started food rationing after the
outbreak of the Pacific War in l94l and that food was
distributed according to need, with manual workers receiving
more than students and teachers. Each student receives
less than a quarter of a pound of rice a day, which is
enough for one meal, and a small cash allowance, which is
enough for only a few biscuits.
Although Japan produces large quantities of bean curd
the students get none of it for bean curd now is used for
industrial purposes. Fish is available twice a month but
a small fish may be divided among as many as fifteen families.
The last pork that Mr. Ying had in Tokyo was at a New Year's
dinner party and the amount was insignificant.
Because of the shortage of food students in Japan take
little exeroise. Many of them go to bed early in the hope
that they can sleep and avoid the pangs of hunger.
When they do have a full meal it is likely to be of
some food like whale meat, which is now popular in Tokyo.
Whale skin shoes, despite their inferior quality, also are
popular, as the Japanese Army is using all ordinary leather.
Most Japanese wear worn wooden sandals and are likely
to have holes in their sooks, as the sook ration is six
pairs a year. Three towels per person can be purchased
each year. Kimonas, instead of falling almost to the
ground, do not cover the knees. Soap is strictly rationed,
three persons sharing one cake of soap per month. This
allotment is so small that soap rarely is used for bathing.
Metals are collected everywhere by local officials.
Window bars, iron gates, hardware and even nails are taken
from houses to add to the sorap piles. Buddhists have
surrendered their saored bells and statues.
H12

=== Page 229 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
High school students have been used to dig slit
trenohes for air raid shelters in the cities. The
trenches looked to Mr. Ying like long grave openings.
Students also are used in labor corps on the farms, as
so many farmers have been sent to the front as soldiers.
Letters sent abroad are subject to striot oensorship.
Nothing can be said in them about the shortage of commodities.
The attitude of the Japanese people towards the war
varies from time to time. The attitude of Japanese students
and professors was optimistic when the Germans were driving
on Moscow, when Rommel was successful in North Africa, and
during the early stages of the Solomons campaign. The
Japanese defeats in the Solomons hurt morale and the loss
of Attu and Kiska was a severe blow. The Japanese had
given new names to these Aloutian islands and were listing
them as Japanese territory.
Mr. Ying says that during the last few months of his
stay in Tokyo the Japanese were becoming more and more
gloomy and that more and more people, including students
and professors, were being arrested for suoh crimes as
"dangerous thoughts." Types of thinking which the authorities
regard as dangerous become especially prevalent after a blow
to morale such as that of the death of Admiral Isoroku
Yamamoto, who was regarded in Japan as another Admiral Togo.
The Japanese like to drink and those who have lost
their enthusiasm for war beoause of the Japanese reverses
in the Pacifio are particularly anxious to have the solace
of wine. But wine is rationed and the shops frequently
sell out their supplies while long lines of customers
still wait outside.
