Enolosure no. 2 to despatch no. 2271 of March 7, 1944
from the American Embassy, Chungking.
China Information Committee
Daily Bulletin -- No. 893
February 28, 1944
TOKYO SIDELIGHTS
At least two-thirds of the inhabitants in Tokyo
today are feminine. The males are outnumbered by women
and girls of all ages everywhere one goes.
The Japanese people have been fond of sweetmeats and
pastries. But in this long-drawn-out war they are no
longer able to satisfy their sweet tooth. Up and down
the streets of Tokyo there are no confectionaries or
bakeries.whGone are the attractive boxes of candies, cookies
and cakes/used to be displayed in the shop-windowe. Sugar,
candy and biscuits are rationed and only occasionally can
Tokyo residents get any of these foods.
Most of the shoppers in Tokyo, in waiting for their
turn to buy the things they need, have to hold ration
coupons in one hand and money in the other.
These are some of the impressions of daily life on
the Japanese home front related by a Chinese who recently
came to the interior from Japan through the occupied areas.
He had been in Tokyo for several years. He told of
rapid changes in the living conditions and daily lives of
the Japanese as compared with pre-war standards. He said
it is now common to see people of all ages walking barefooted
in the streets of Tokyo on rainy days.
The asphalt road surfaces have worn out, he said, so
cinders have been used to fill up the numerous holes.
Japanese wartime shoes as a rule do not last long on cinder
roads. To save their precious shoes many Japanese have
started walking bare-footed when it rains so that they will
have shoes to wear on good days.
House-to-house census taking has become more and more
frequent. The questionings and surveys are mainly in
connection with conscription and ration coupons. After
the drafting of practically all able bodied men into the
armed servioes, even men above the age of 58 have been
called to serve in the "Citizens Militia Corps."
The shortage of foods has turned most of the gardens
in Tokyo, including small back-yard gardens, into vegetable
patohos.

=== Page 231 === (confidence 0.99)
2
Eggs are rarely seen in the market. If a siok man
or convalescent has to have eggs for food, he must produce
a dootor's certificate.
To the Japanese soy sauce is an essential item in
diet. But even soy sauce has been rationed. A Japanese
is now able to get only half as much soy sauce as he has
been accustomed to consume. Meat has become a lurury as
it has been striotly rationed. A Japanese civilian gets
meat about once a month.
There is, however, still one way of getting chicken
and eggs to eat. It is sometimes possible to get in a
restaurant a bowl of steamed rice with a few slices of
half-cooked chicken and eggs on top. This is a favorite
Japanese dish and is now more popular than ever.
Restaurants are said to be doing a roaring business--
as long as their supply of foods lasts. The prosperity
of restaurant business in Japan is unprecedented. But
restaurants cannot buy all the provisions they want or
used to get. There is a general restriction on the amount
of rice each customer is allowed to eat. At present no
customer is served more than a couple of ounces of rice.
In order to get enough to eat one has to eat in at least
three restaurants, going directly from one place to another.
This is by no means easy because all the restaurants are
crowded and usually sell all the food they have for one
meal in less than an hour's time.
Traveling is most difficult. The railways are mostly
reserved for troop transportation. Strict restrictions
are imposed on non-military personnel. A civilian desiring
to travel from one place to another by train must first
register his intention, destination and purpose. If his
application is approved he will be specifically assigned
to a certain train on a certain day. Japanese trains today
carry more passengers than the designed capacity. As the
cars are always jammed many passengers used to have to
stand all the way no matter how far they were going. As
a partial solution to this problem, the authorities have
adopted a "seat changing system." A signal is given at
regular intervals in the trains. When the signal is sounded,
all those who have been sitting are obliged to stand up
and let those who have been standing take the seats.

SubjecT: CONDITIONS IN JAPAN.
Source. Mr. Ch'ih Ch'ien-yuan, a Chinese student
from Peiping, who states that he has completed four years'
medical study at Keio University, situated in a Tokyo
suburb. Mr. Ch'ih stated that he left Japan September 20,
1943 and that his reasons for leaving Japan were (1 fear
of forceful induction into the Japanese Army medical
corps, and (2 inability to obtain further setisfactory
education and training ut Kcio Uriversity, the medical
and other classes having been virtually denuded of students
by the drafting of Jupunese youth for military service.
Health of Japanese. Mr. Ch'ih stated that during
the six months immediately precedine his departure from
Japan, he served as an interne in a Tokyo hospital. He
stated that it is his observation that there is an acute
shortage of doetore md tr ined ursus in Jupan, us well
as at the fichtiag fronts. He lso ste ted that shortages
of medicines exist in Jap an. He exprensed the view that
Japanese-trained doctors ure inferior o those trained
in Western institutione or formerly tr ined in the Peiping
Union Medical Colloge. He remurked that Japanese hospitals
are by no means so sunitury us mi ght be supposed, being
below mission-orerated hospitals in China in hygienic
standards. He asserted that thore is a large number of
military hospitals in Japan which re illed with Japanese
wounded.
Mr. Ch'ih said that the incidence of tuberculosis in
Japan, always high in that country, is increasine at a
marked rate. Ho attributed this phenomenon to two muin
factors: (1 improper diet nd malnutrition of the great
bulk of the ropulation, and (2 overwork, strain und
anxiety. He rem.rked that the hospitals are overflowing
with tubereulosis putients. Mr. Ch'ih usserted that the
second great disease of Japun is syphilis and thit its
inroads on the people is very great.
Mr. Ch'ih remurked that the present-day Japanese diet
is inadequate. He asserted that sweet potatoes are now
a staple of the Japanese diet. He said that the Japanese
people never see meat and that while the supply of fish
is ample in certain areas such as Tokyo many ureas are
very short of this important food. He said that there
was no soarcity of vegetables. As regards rice, he
asserted that it is strictly rationed; each person is
limited to two small bowls per meal (say less than half
the average consumption in normal times). Nr. Ch ih
stated that the restaurants, which are always crowded to
overflowing, are open only at stated intervals, that is,
between 11 a.m. and 2 p.m. and 5 p.m. and 8 p.m.
General conditions. Mr. Ch'ih said it was his
observation that the Japanese people are beginning more
and more to feel the impact of prolonged warfare in every
Bphoro

=== Page 235 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
sphere of life. In this relation, he commented on
various factors such as malnutrition and increased
incidence of disease (as related above), the growing
shortage of consumer commodities, the increasingly
pressing labor problem, the growing fear of air raids,
and a growing consciousness mong the Japanese people
of the inability of the Japanese armed forces to defeat
their adversaries. Mr. Ch ih said that he regarded the
labor problem in Japan to be very acute. He said that
hours of work in the factories, vhich are devoted almost
exclusively to war production, are now very long. In
important factories operation is on a 24-hour basis
comprising a day and a night shift. Most war workers,
Mr. Ch'ih asserted, work a 12-hour shift with perhaps
one hour of this period devoted to relaxation and meals.
He added that workers are not given holidays. Like
other observers, Mr. Ch'ih remarked that Japanese women
have virtually taken over all unessential occupations;
in addition, they are working to some extent in the factories
and in heavy coolie labor. Japanese women are now a mainstay
of domestic agriculture. Nr. Ch'ih confirmed that the
migration of Korean agricultural lubor to Japanese farms
has been heavy.

=== Page 238 === (confidence 0.99)
Report on Interview with a Chinese woman who left Japan
at the end of October 1943.
I.
Foreword
The following miscellaneous information was provided by
a young Chinese woman who left Shanghai, September 25, 1943,
spent almost a month in Japan, and returned to Shanghai. Sub-
sequently she left Shanghai and came to Chungking.
II.
Reliability
The reliability of the informant cannot be accurately
judged. Possibly she is entirely reliable. By her own
statement she is well acquainted with responsible officials
of both the Free China Government and the Government of
occupied China. In Japan she was received in the home of a
middle-class Japanese family. While such a situation may seem
incomprehensible to some Americans, it is not unusual in China,
and such relationships do not necessarily mean that the in-
formant cannot be trusted.
As to the information provided, it may be generally
evaluated as "probably true". Some of it has been confirmed
by other sources, and all of it seems reasonable, in view of
other information.
III.
Routes Followed by Informant
Informant left Shangha1 on September 25, 1943, as a
passenger on a Japanese ship (the name of which she says she
cannot recall). The ship on which she traveled was one of
three similar ships in a convoy, protected by one submarine,
and one aeroplane which circled overhead. No prior announce-
ment had been made of the sailing. Two of the ships in the
convoy went to Nagasaki. The one on which informant was a
passenger entered the Inland Sea via Shimonoseki Strait and
anchored off Moji. Late in the afternoon the ships left
Moji for Kobe, and all passengers were required to stay in
their cabins for about one hour and one-half. Kobe was
reached the following morning.
Leaving Kobe by local train at 4 p.m., informant sat up
all night in a coach, and arrived at Yokohama at 6 a.m. the
xt morning. After breakfast with friends in Yokohama,
Tormant took the electric train for Tokyo at l0 a.m.,
iving at Central Station "about forty minutes" later.
When she left Tokyo, she took the "Fuji" Express from
8 a.m. the next morning. Leaving by the Shimoneseki-Fusan
teok the Mght train from Fusan to Peking via Mukden. The
trip from aking te shanghai was made by air.
IV.

=== Page 239 === (confidence 0.99)
IV.
Comments by Informant on Miscellaneous Subjects, as follows:
TRANSPORTATION
Railways
The "Fuji Express" leaves Central Station, Tokyo at 1 p.m.,
daily. It arrives at Shimonoseki at 8 a.m., the following
morning. The train carries second class sleeping cars and a
diner. A local train leaves Kobe at 4 p.m., and arrives at
Yokohama at 6 a.m., the following morning. There are no sleep-
ing cars and no diner on this train. Electric trains from
Yokohama to Tokyo make the run in "about forty minutes". All
trains in Japan are very crowded. Trains in Korea are "very
dirty and poorly maintained".
Ships
The Shimonoseki-Fusan ferry leaves Shimonoseki soon
after 8 a.m., runs a zig-zag course all day, and reaches
Fusan "about dark" (November lst). The ferry itself was des-
oribed by informant as a "small" ship. The night runs by the
Shimonoseki-Fusan ferries have been discontinued.
No "big" ships were seen at Shimonoseki, Moji, Kobe, or
Yokohama. "A few small ships" were noted. No war ships of
any kind were seen in the ports named, nor in crossing from
Shimonoseki to Fusan.
Local Street Railways
Street sars in Tokyo stop running at about 9 p.m. and
there is no service until morning.
Automobiles
Some official automobiles and a few private cars were
seen operating in Tokyo. Informant believes they were burn-
ing gasoline. Taxicabs are not numerous and they burn char-
coal. Taxis are available only at certain places, such as
Contral Station and the Imperial Hotel. It is very diffi-
cult to find one elsewhere. Prospective passengers line up
with their baggage and wait their turn for taxis.
AIR RAID PRECAUTIONS
Tokyo is blacked out every night. There are no street
lights, and lights from shops, restaurants, movies, etc., do
not show in the streets. Informant reported that the black-
out exists throughout Japan and Korea. The oity of Antung
is also blacked out. Elsewhere in Manchuria and North China,
however, there is no black out. In riding through Japan and
Korea at night by train, no lights whatever can be seen in
the country or in villages.
Throughout Tokyo, "Air Raid Trenches" have been dug.
These are plain ditehes, "about five feet deep and leng
mough for four persons to squat in". When asked how many
suek tremehes have been provided, informant replied, "Oh,
plemty of them".
COMUNICATIONS ....

=== Page 240 === (confidence 0.99)
COMMUNICATIONS
Radios
Informant stated that there are no short-wave radio sets
available to the people in Japan and that there is no oppor-
tunity for them to hear foreign broadcasts. In Shanghai,
however, she stated that "lots of people have short-wave sets
and listen to foreign stations regularly, though they are not
supposed to".
PRICES AND COST OF LIVING
Hotel Rates
Informant paid 20 Yen per day for a room, with meals
included, at the Imperial Hotel in Tokyo. At Miyanoshita,
she paid 22 Yen per day for room and meals at the Fujiya Hotel.
Cigarette Prices
"Sakura" brand cigarettes were sold at 55 sen for 10
cigarettes, when an empty package was returned.
Clothing Prices
Informant purchased one kimono of pure silk, lined with
silk of poorer quality, for 22 Yen. She described this as a
"good" kimono. She also purchased a "cheaper" kimono, which
was unlined, for 15 yen.
RATIONING
As a visitor, informant had no ration tickets and could
not make purchases in shops. To overcome this she would pick
out what she wanted and have it bought for her by German
acquaintances in the hotel, who "seemed to be able to buy
things".
FOOD
As a visitor and guest, living in Japanese hotels, in-
formant had no ration tickets and required none. It was also
possible for her to eat in restaurants when she desired with-
out ration tickets. However, she described such restaurant
meals as "unsatisfactory because the portions are so mall
you are still hungry when you have eaten everything".
Meals are still being served in the Imperial Hotel dining
room, and in the basement grill there. The hotel is well
filled with Japanese and German business men, government
offieials, etc. She heard many of them complain that they
were not getting enough to eat and were "always hungry".
At a special breakfast served to informant by friends in
Yokohama, in their own home, the memu consisted of Japanese
pears, riee and tea. Asked whether the riee was white or
brewm, she stated definitely that it was "polished Japanese
riee and very white".
Informent ....

=== Page 241 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
Informant stated that she heard there is a black market
in Tokyo and that almost anything is obtainable if you have
enough money. Those who have Chinese cooks seem to be faring
best and there is a saying in Tokyo, "To eat well, get a
Chinese cook".
MISCELLANEOUS
Entertainment
"Thore are lots of movies running in Tokyo and many plays,
but no cabarets". The movies show mostly war pictures and
propaganda films.
"Briefing" for Tokyo Visit
Before she was permitted to leave Shanghai, informant was
told that she should not talk about Shanghai, or conditions
thore, while she was in Tokyo, and had to agree to this stipu-
lation before she was given her travel papers. Informant
interpreted this requirement as an effort to keep people in
Tokyo from knowing that there is more food and generally
better conditions in Shanghai than in Japan.

I am most grateful for your advice
and hope you ill permit us not only to inform
you of our progress, but also to profit from
your counsel.
Sincerely,
Nuh Dunn

July 19, 1944
MEMORANDUM FOR FILES
Japanese Army Returns Businesses to Puppet
Philippine Republic
A radio broadcast from Manila on July llth states that the
Japanese Army in the Philippines had transferred to the "Govern-
mont of the Republic of the Philippines" a total of 24 Government-
owned or other industrial companies which heretofore had been ad-
ministered by the Japanese Army. The companies were listed as
follows:
894.00/7-1944
National Development Company
2.
National Abaca and Other Fibers Corporation
3.
National Coconut Corporation
4.
National Food Products Corporation
5.
National Warehousing Corporation
6.
National Footwear Corporation
7.
National Tobacco Corporation
8.
National Power Corporation
9.
Manila Railroad Company
10.
Peoples Homesite Corporation
11.
Cebu Portland Cement Company
12. Insular Sugar Refining Company
All of the above are organizations established by the Phil-
ippine Commonwealth Government.
13. Telegraph Enterprise of Bureau of Posts
14. Telephone Administration of Mountain Province and
NOV 15 1
Baguio City
15. Baguio Municipal Electric Enterprise
FIL
16. City of Manila Tramcar Enterprise
17. Manila Gas Company
196
S
The last two above were not Government enterfrises, the
H
Manila Tramoar Enterprise having been operated by the Manila
Eleotric Company, an American corporation, and the Manila Gas
Company also was American owned.
18. Philippine Sugar Association
19. Philippine Salt Control Association
20. Philippine Leaf Tobacco Association
21. Manila Tobacoo Association
22. Philippine
cen

=== Page 249 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
22. Philippine Fertilizer Distribution Association
Philippine Shipping Association
Philippine Prime Commodities Control Association
Mone of the above were actually government enterprises.
The Philippine Sugar Association and the Manila Tobacco Associa-
tion were private trade associations before the war. All of the
rest were established by the Japanese Military Administration
after Japanese occupation. Ostensibly they were private trade
associations but actually were instrumentalities of the Japanese
organized to oontrol production and distribution of the various
commodities. All had Japanese advisers and were really Japanese
oontrolled.
The announcer, Arsenio Luz, spokesman of the Board of Infor-
mation, stresses the transfer of the properties as "a most sig-
nifioant step taken by the Imperial Japanese Army in building up
the new Government." He states further that "as a spontaneous
action on the part of the Republic" and as a mark of gratitude
and in pursuance of the pact of alliance the "Republic has invited
the Imperial Japanese Army to continue to operate the undertakings
which it considers essential to the Greater East Asia war". It
develops that the Japanese Army "graciously consented" to continue
to operate the first seventeen enterprises, leaving to the
"Republio" only the seven trade associations, which, as pre-
viously indioated are entirely under Japanese control.
This represents the ultimate in empty gestures and is very
poor propaganda since it is obvious that it represents merely the
return of nominal title to enterprises whioh were seized by the
Japanese Army. Nevertheless, the Minister of Finance in the
puppet Government, Antonio de las Alas, 1s reported to have waxed
oloquent in appreciation of the gratitude of the Filipino people
for the kindness and benevolence of the Japanese Nation. He
states:
"It 1s a great pleasure for us to be able to lend for the
time being many of these enterprises to the Imperial
Japanese Army in fulfillment of the terms of the Paot of
Alliance but also as an act of gratitude on our part in
return for the benevolence and kindness shown to us on
many ocoasions by the Imperial Japanese Army."
The radio report fails to state whether the speaker choked while
uttering these noble sentiments.

"Let us examine the proposed area of what i
Depertment of State
the Greater East Asia Co-Prosperity Sphere, as envisioned
by the Japanese. Broadeasting from Chungking, on Kay 19th,
Dr. T. Chiang, a Government spokesman, tells of a map pub-
l1shed in June, l941, with the sanotion of the Imperial
Japanese Government. It shows the sphere as inoluding
the whole of continental Asia, with the sole exception of
Arabia. The boundary-line ertends through the Persian
Gulf to the Caspåan Sea, temohing the great oil-fields at
Baku. Thenoe it runs northeasterly to the foot of the
Ural Mountains. It inoludes all of China, Indo-China
Thailand, the Malay Peninsula, Burma, the Dutch East Indies,
New Guinea, all of Sakhalin, the Philippines, the northern
tip of Australia including Darwin and Cape York, together
0
with the vast ocean areas and the many groups of Islands,
Q
including Guam and Yap. "The extent of Japan's sphere,"
says Dr. Chiang, 'stops short of continental Australia and
does not include the Hawaiian Islands, though mention has
been made of Alaska to be added within the sphere.'
"In part confirmation of this ambitious statement, a
8
Tokyo broadoast in Japanese on May 17th, states that the
area of the GEA Co-Prosperity sphere will embrace one
quarter of the entire globe, including India, Australia
and New Zealand. It points out that, in the GEA area
already conquered, Japan has all of the resources needed
to make her entirely self-sufficient, while America and
Britain have been deprived of vital and much-needed materials...
"At the second meeting of the Greater East Asia Inquiry
Board, held in Tokyo on May 4th, 1942, the following outline
of fundamental ideals was accepted:
"1. The GEA establishment is to be based upon the
spirit of the Japanese national structure, under the super-
vision of the military administration.
"2. The countries concerned shall have their natural
resources developed and extended in mutual oo-operation to
inerease the total eeonomio strength of the GEA.
"3. Japan

=== Page 263 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
"3. Japan must foster an interest in international
relations and loyalty so that complete internal reorgani-
sation of these countries may be attained.
"4. Peoples within the GEA sphere must realize that
the fate of the GxA rests upon co-operation with the
military administration.
"5. The whole struoture must be departmentalized
into Industry and Produotion, Finance, Export Trade and
Soience. Special researches must be undertaken upon
infectious diseases, mineral resouroes, agricultural
produots and the transportation problems of the GkA area.
"6. Tours of industry must be organized for University
students, and a special GEA Eoonomics Section must be set
up to handle educational publicity through lectures, motion-
pioture films and similar methods...
"Within the last two months the politioal system of
Japan has been entirely revised, resulting in complete con-
trol both of politics and economics by the military-
capitalistio-bureauoratio elements of the nation. While
there has been a steady trend in this direotion for a
deoade or more, it has never been unopposed. Nothing
approaching the conditions brought about by a recent eleo-
tion for the imperial Diet has existed since feudalism was
swept away &o years ago. While it is of no ∞ncern at
present how long suoh conditions will continue in the face
of ultimate viotory or defeat, it seems vitally important
that Amerioans understand how Japan has reorganized and
centralized itself to meet the war threat. Here is given
a brief explanatory baokground and the various steps by
whioh the reorganization has been accomplished since the
beginning of 1942, with something of the economio results
already apparent.
"... There had been no election to the Diet sinoe the
spring of 1937 before the China Inoident began. The old
Diet had been under sufficient control to vote the huge
budgets required by the military for carrying on the China
war and preparing for the greater war to come. But it could
not be depended upon to support the sweeping changes now
planned, and doubtless it was deemed necessary to get the
support of the nation behind these changes by means of an
eleetion. It was announced, therefore, that one would be
held at the end of April. (First announcement of this must
havo

=== Page 264 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
have been in February, for on the 23rd of that month,
accerding to a broadoast made Maroh 22, Premier Tojo
called together thirty-three national leaders and explained
the plans to them.)
"As these plans are now seen in retrospect, it appears
that the GEA concept, formerly a foreign polioy, had been
turned into the basis and rallying point of the reorganized
state. It was capable of appealing to the glory and greed
of the nation and was made palatable to the idealists by
the #co-prosperity sphere" promise. It served adequately
as the single and all-embracing plank of the IRAA, now
promoting and managing the eleotion. The election chairman
of this organization, General Nobuyuke Abe, former premier,
speaking in Japanese over the radio Maroh 22, is quoted as
saying to the organization's branch leaders:
"'Your first duty is to instill among the people a
strong stimulus toward fulfillment of the saored war,
thereby showing the true spirit of assisting the Imperial
rule and establishing a strong IRaA political struoture of
the nation.' (Translation by Station Ponda.) He went on
to reveal the way in which the government intended to gain
control of the Diet, which was simply by hand-pioking the
candidates, given the euphonius name of 'endorsing them.
Ho oontinuod:
"'In the past, candidates have been permitted to regis-
ter without any difrioulty ... Unquestionably there were
those eleoted who had every intenéion of giving all help to
the government ... but they (former Diet members) have been
an obstaole in the formation of the IRAA politioal struoture ...
(To ohoose men for its purpose) the IRAA political counoil
must carry on a detailed study of each candidate and then
conoentrate on the election (of those endorsed).' Abe then
asked for as many qualified men as possible to come forward,
that the seleotions might be wide...
"Fifteen million voters were qualified to go to the
polls on eleotion day, April 30. IRAA-endorsed candidates
won 387 out of the 477 seats in the House of Representatives.
There were 88/lidorsed candidates elected, or 22 per cent of
the membership, a significant number under the ciroumstanees.
Of those elected, 200 were new men taking their places for
the first time, and of this group l68 were endorsed by the
DRAA

=== Page 265 === (confidence 0.99)
-4
IRAA (May 2).
"Eiection practices were somewhat improved over the
past, but still bad enough. In 1937 there had been 978
eases of violations (meaning vote selling, bribery and
worse), involving 1,827 people; in this election there
were only 431 cases involving 1,176 people (May 12).
"THE DMPZRIAL RULE ASSISTANCE POLITICAL SOCIETY
"With a large majority of the House behind him, over
half of whom were new members not conversant with parlia-
mentary taotios, and an enthusiastic nation, Premier Tojo
proceeded immediately after the election to embark on a
series of political moves which can have no parallel in
modern political history. To understand these moves fully,
it must be kept in mind, as already stated, that the Con-
stitution is sacrosanct, its provisions to be fulfilled
to the letter. But the letter is enough; formal recogni-
tion is about all the Japanese emperors have enjoyed for
centuries. In flousing the spirit of the Constitution and
substituting government by a olique, Tojo and his supporters
are acting direotly in the main tradition of Japanese poli-
tical procedure and are incurring no serious criticism for
so doing.
"His first step, May 4, was to announce t hat an extra-
ordinary session of the Diet would be convened May 27, 28
and 29. His second, May 7, was to call together a group of
77 men, leaders in finance, government, various influential
organizations, the army and navy. To these he explained
his ideas and asked them to serve as a supporting nuoleus
in putting them into effeot. It may be assumed with cer-
tainty that each man had long been previously aware of the
plan in general and was ready to act. The speed of what
followed indicates that carefully laid plans were being
followed.
" ... The meaning of national unity as a mall, one-
group control of national power and finance was revealed
suddenly on May 20, when, with a great burst of explana-
tions, there was announced the inaugural meeting on that
date of the new Imperial Rule Assistance Political Society
(Taisei Yokusan Selji Kai).
"This was made up of a select group chosen from the
original IRaA. The latter obviously, had grown too large
and held too many discordant elements, such as former
politioal

=== Page 266 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
politieal leaders who had climbed on its bandwagon and
would be inolined to wrangle, real liberals, and plain
Sethers.* The original IRAA still continues in existence
as a large supporting group, and even attracts new nem-
bership as various smaller groups hasten to join up since
its sweeping success in the elections. Such for instance,
was the Tohokai, which dissolved and its membership was
taken into the fRAA and six of its officers made a part
of the select IRA Political Society (May 23).
"Invitations to join this select group were sent to
1044 people. They included the entire membership of the
new House of Representatives, the House of Peers, 14 mem-
bers of the Imperial household, all cabinet members, and
a group of important board and bureau heads. Of the num-
ber invited 23 persons declined outright to join and 89
others remained indeterminate by nos answering. (This is
opnsidered a more polite way of refusing than by a defin-
ite answer.) These 112 probably represent most of the
opposition to this movement in important places, and doubt-
less include many of the non-endorsed members of the House
of Representatives. Attending the inaugural meeting,
finally, were 933 persons which inoluded all of the cabinet
members except the Army and Navy Ministers who, for some
reason, did not join the organization (May 20).
"In the two weeks intervening between the first meet-
ing of the 77 and the inaugural meeting of the new poli-
tieal society, the 77 had made themselves into a central
counoil. It is obvious that in these 77 lies the om ef
support of the movement. They had eleoted directors and
standing directors, formed committees and named chairmen,
building up an extended framework for a powerful politioal
organization.
"(The 29 direotors named on the recommendation of
President Abe are: Michitomo Okuro, Sotaro Ishiwata,
Torotaro Ishizuka, Ryusaku Endo, Tadahiko Odada, Tadao
Oeata, Koso Ohota, Masatake Uota, Nagatani Okabe, Masanori
(Kusatsu), Isuneo Hanamitsu, Iohiro Kiyose, Kuniya Saito,

Masataka Ota, Nawakami Okabe, Fumio Goto, Takuo Godo,
Jyutaro Nagai, Yonezo Mayeda, Tatsunosuke Yamazaki and
Fukenari Yokoyama. (Ponda, May 20).
"General Abe was eleoted president at the inaugural
meeting and Fumio Goto was made a sort of executive
seeretary. (Apparently he and Tatsunosuke Yamazaki had
been acting in this oapacity, doing the hard work of
organizing, for when it was all over they paid a thankful
visit to Meiji shrine.)
"The platform of the new organization, as given in the
English broadoast of May 20, states:
"'First, resolutely to proceed with the attainment of
the successful conolusion of the GEA war by consolidating
national political power in accordance with the essential
prinoiples of the national policy. Second, fimmly to estab-
lish the IRAPS Diet, in strict accordanoe with the provisions
of the Imperial Constitution, Third, to attain (firmness)
of the Imperial Rule Assistance movement, in olose allegiance
and cooperation with the IRAA. Fourth, to make efforts to
construot a new order of the world by setting up the GKA
co-prosperity sphere.'
"This resume, intended for foreign consumption, tells
very little. The ultimate object of the organization is
oontrol both of the politics and the economics of the
nation...
"Among the things accomplished before the Diet met was
the seleotion of the president and vice-president of the
House. These two were seleoted by Abe, but formally elected
at the Diet meeting. More important was the new way devised
for handling bills to be introduced into the House. A single
group hereafter will study all proposed bills, this group
replacing several whioh formerly funotioned on this matter.
Needless to say, in passing through this 'unified' channel,
only those bills approved by the IRapS will ever reach the
membership.
"While the election gave control of the House of Repre-
sentatives to the IRAPB, oontrol of the permanent House of
Peers required other tactics. These did not involve the
publie, however, so that little is heard about them. It
was anmounoed on May 25, that various factions in the House
of Peers had agreed to form coordinating committees to work
吐

=== Page 268 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
with the IRAPS, so that for the present the Peers have
lined up with the controlling clique. Another means by
which the House of Peers was brought in line was by the
appointment of Abe to a seat in that body. As a member
he will be in a much better position to exercise needed
influence.
"The Diet actually opened on May 25, two days before
its ceremonious opening on May 27, with the Emperor present
as usual. During these two preliminary days members were
evidently seated and instructed in procedure. Without
parties on whioh to base seating arrangements, in the House,
a geographioal plan was followed. Prefectures were grouped
together in sequence, those at one end of the country on one
side, those from the other end on the opposite side. (May 23.)
"Five bills were presented to this Diet, three of them
on budgetary matters, two of them having to do with ship-
building. An additional budget of Yen 12,846,000 was asked
to inorease facilities for the training of seamen, and to
finanoe a GEA Construction Inquiry Board. The non-budgetary
bills provided ways and means for building ships more
rapidly, by subsidy and supply of materials. One bill also
had something to do with wage increases in industrial plants.
"SUMMARY
"Within the last two months Japan has oompletely
revamped its political organization, but has done this
entirely within the legal framework of the Constitution
and the Diet. By winning a one-party eleotion and organiz-
ing the Imperial Rule Assistance Politioal Society, the
controlling government clique made up of military, oapital-
istic, and bureaucratic factions has now achieved a strangle-
hold on the country. Faced by the threat of the war and
the hope of exploiting the southern regions, the people are
willing at the moment that this should be so. Thus the
Greater East Asia Co-prosperity Sphere has been given the
"Green Light" by the people of Japan, who now fervently
hope that it will work.

I should like to present for consideration by the Department and for
discussion next Thursday afternoon the following:
1. The attached memorandum originating in New York and addressed to
Mr. Roberts, Radio Production head, from Mr. Delgado -- a study proposing an
alternative series of programs on.inter-American cooperation to be done jointly
by the Department and the Coordinator.
You will recall the August 10 meeting in Mr. Beggs' office at which
time it was agreed that we submit to the Department several proposals for such
a series.
 Considerable discussion between this Office and the Radio Production
Office in New York seems to indicate the inadvisability, from a radio point of
view, of a "Buzz Sawyer" type of program illustrating the nature of the Japs.
We should like to present for reconsideration, therefore, a modification of the
original "La Mascara Sonriente" show. In this new series it is proposed that
the locale of operations be some Jap-occupied area so that adequate display can
be given to the effect of Jap occupation on the life, customs, morale and treat-
ment of the local inhabitants. This device of the locale will give, it is be-
lieved, an opportunity to point out the nature of the enemy in ali the necessary
facsimile detail. As to structure ana  lot, there has been suggested by New
York for your consideration either : running documentary on village life or a
"soap box opera" documentary-dramatic. Several areas have been under considera-
tion -- Burma, China, the Philippines, Manchukuo, Siam. It is further queried
by New York as to the. possibility of revolving this around a typical manufac-
tured underground activity. It is felt that ample documentation can be obtained
and that an authoritative note couid be lent through a possible cooperation of
script writers and researchers with appropriate members of the Department.

Having left Kobe on the 17th of July, the former
Iwiss Consul at Kobe, Maurice Champoud, has just arrived
via the Soviet Union, in Ankara. He gave the following
information on Japanese conditions:
Great numbers of the population of Tokyo have moved
into the interior. Nearly all wooden buildings in Tokyo
have been demolished. Japan and Japanese-controlled
territories are suffering from the improper distribution
of supplies due to regulations prohibiting the transfer
of foodstuffs from one prefecture to another. The
disorganized system of transportation is also responsible
for this condition. The two daily meals leave most of
the Japanese population hungry. Although details are not
furnished with regard to losses ships, planes, or men,
the Japanese people are furnished with information concern-
ing military reverses. Champoud has no authentio infor-
mation concerning Japanese production. His statements
on this subject would merely be surmises. The belief
in Japan's final victory is still held by many Japanese
espeoially the military, but no one seems to be able
to give an explanation of how this 1s to be attained.

BELLISH LIFE FOR WOMEN IN JAPANESE FARN VILLAGES.
BY.NARS. DOto.?
The aggressive war of Japanese Fascists, like a plague, has
swept away the mmjority of able-bodied young men in Japanese farm
willages. Those remaining behind are mainly women, old-aged men,
md children. Thus, the life of Japanese women in far villages,
whe had already been leading an animal life before the war, has
beseme the more painful.
In the November 1943 issue of the Japanese N WOOLD
Magesine, the minutes of a farm village women's symposium was pub-
llahed. This mymposium was held in the (INokuc I ( ) vil-

 PURCHASING THOOP"-A OLIMPSE OF THE NATIONAL LIFE IN JAPAN
In present-day JAPAn, despite the urging of the eating of
cearse rice or miscellaneous grains, there is a shortage of cureals.
Im the c1t1es, only 2.26 g0 (approximately 0.71868 pint, or 0.40se
litse) of cearse rice ls rationed to each adult dally. so for each
meal each person can hardly get one bowl of cooked rice. People
feel humgry immediately after they have finished their meal. The
sitmmtion is especlally awful for the children and t'e youngsters
e are just at the age of physical development. Their digeation
beeemes poor when they eat the coarse rice. ost of ther: suffer
frm digostive troubles.  his ls not only true with the foodstuffs;
fer even the ration of vegetables and fish is smal. Consequently
people living in the citles are compelled to find some other way
to get their food. Thus, the so-called "Purchasing roop" came into
being.
sandays and holidays are the days of mobilization of these
"Purchasing Troops". On such days, the trolley-cars from OxYo to
the outskirts are packed tignt full. Men and women of all classes,
young and old, middle and primary school atudents, are jam ed in
the cars of the trains. ven the tollets are ful  of people sitting
 papere put on the floor. Although their countenancos and clothes
are dlfferont from one another, there is one similarity, which is
that they are all carryin baga and baskete. They go to the country
farm houses early in the morning to buy rice, beans, and vegetables,
mave them packed in the bags and baskets, and come back to the city
with the harvest of their whole day's labour by the evening trolley-
ears. One writer desoribes humorously this kind of "purohasing
twains" In the following way: "Wether it is a fine or a rainy day,
as seon as you step Into the car you will find the scent of vegetables
pemetrating into your nose. The other day when I stepped into the
eas I thought I was mistakenly taking the train for vegetables and
froight. Am it was so crowded that ry fet felt numb, I lifted one
f them to change the position I had taken. But as soon as I had
lifted it I found no place to put it down again, not even the little
apase it had oocupied the mament befere. Then I had to stand on ene
feet all the time. Mereover, members of the "purchasing troop"
arownd me kept a suapiclous and frightened eye on the man who was
empty handed. I was constantly kmocked on the breast by radish and
 myback by burdook.

The Program of the Japanese Communist Party.
Commanding General, Fwd. Eoh. USAF-CBI, APO 879
1. There ere attached notes of a conversation with Okano
 on September 8, 1944, in which Mr. Okano outlined in
detail the present program of the Japanese Communist Party.
2. A brief biographical sketoh of Mr. Okano has been
peepared by Mr. F. M. Fisher who it is understood will furnish
Sopies to the Headquarters and Embassy.
3. 1t is impossible to know how authoritatively and accurately
M. Okamo now speaks for the Communist Party within Japan. He
left that country in l932 as the representative of the Japanese
Communist Party to the Third Internationale. In 1935 while
la Mosoow he was elected a member of the rresidium of the
Executive Committee of the Third Internationale. He reached
Yenan in the Spring of 1943 (by means which he does not disouss)
and is regarded here as a representative of the Central committee
of the Japanese Communist Party. Although he has not been in
Japen for 12 years, there is occasional underground contaot
between the Japanese in Yenan and Communist groups within
Japan.
4. The program described by Mr. Okano is interesting for its
moderate character and its conoeption of the progress of Japan
toward socialism by a gradual development through political
demoeraoy and demoorasically controlled capitalism in which
the Communists will ally themselves with any genuinely demooratic
croups.
5. The similarities and parallels between Mr. Okano's
program and that of the Chinese Communist Party is striking.
It is, in fact, the same program with slight modifioations for
speeial conditions and the post-war status of Japan. It would
be interesting to know whether this program owes anything to
Mr. Okano's recent close contact with the Chinese Communist
leaders.
6. The Japanese.Communist Party is still small; Mr. Okano
himselr does not claim more than "a few thousand members". But
it bas the advantages of strong organization and loyal, active
and politioally experienoed membership. Ir its policies are,
as elaimed, congruent with our own hopes for a demooratio,
mom-militaristic Japan, we may wish to consider the adoption
teward it of an attitude of at least sympatatio support as
ome of the more dependably advanoed political groups within
Japan.
7. It is requested that copies of this report be transmitted
te the Ameriean Ambassador at Chungking and Headquarters, USar-CBI,
far the imfermmtion or Mr. Davies.
John S. Servioe

OGRAM OF THE JAPANESE COMMUNIST PARTY
Disoussion by
OKANO Susumu
Sept. 8, 1944
We must first rid Japan of militarism and persistent
foudalistio remnants in politios and economics. After the
establishment of democraoy, we will have a gradual develop-
mont from capitalism to socialism. All this development
should pass through three stages.
TRaT STAGE (Ending of the War and Reorganization of the Country)
I. PROGRAM
Stop work in the factories.
Foree the end of the war by crippling produotion at home.
Bring about peace.
(Okano assumes full surrender to the United Nations.)
B. Overthrow the present militarist government.
Purge the influence of the militarists from the government
and politics.
Punish the militarists and politicians who were responsible
for the war.
C.Demooratize the Government.
1. Institue universal suffrage -- for men and women,
without restriotions or qualirications.
2. Place full governing power in the Diet.
It must be free from the threat of arbitrary dissolution
by other constitutional organs.
It must have full power to initiate and pass legislation.
It should have a Cabinet responsible to it.
3. Foree: the retirement of the present Emperor -- if there
is any successor his constitutional powers must be
reduoed.
The present Emperor cannot escape some personal
responsibility for the war. He did not oppose
it at any stage although such aotion would
have been effeotive if strongly taken, expecially
at an early point in aggression suoh as when the
militarists invaded Manchuria.
4. But for the time being nothing will be done about
abolishing the institution of the Emperor.
We do not believe that the people of Japan are
ready to completely diseard the Emperor; we
are comtent to wait to see, and to be sure that
the time is right before we propose decisive aotion.
lastead we diseuss in our propaganda the nature of
the Emperor and his position. We explain and expose,
without making any direct attaok. We avoid the
slogan: "Down with the Emperor".
We think that any attempt, by us or by the United
Masions to ytilise the Emperor will be dangerous.
It een set an axample whioh may also be used
by groups working against our interests. It is
impersant to remember that it will not be easy
to treat the Emperer in the same mammer as the
British.

=== Page 293 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
British King. The whole problem of the future
of the Emperorship is better left alone for a
while.
5. The Privy Counoil, the House of Peers, and the peouliar
status of the "Elder Statesmen" should be reduced
toward eventual liquidation.
6. Full demooratio rights should be given the people.
These are the ordinary basio rights such as freedom of
speech, thought, expression, person and assembly.
The granting of these rights must mean the end of the
present police government and interference of the
police in the private lives of the oitizens.
7. All political parties must have freedom and legal recognition
This must include the Communist Party.
8. The present Constitution must be revised on the basis
of the above points.
This revision must inolude abolition of the special
powers enjoyed by the military, such as direct
aocess to the Emperor, supra-Cabinet status, and
control of the armed services by active members
of those services.
D. Place large scale monopoly capital under government control.
The war was started by the militarists with the support
of the big munitions manufacturers. The destruction of
the militarists will not be enough unless we also con-
trol their capitalist supporters.
This oontrol does not mean conriscation. This is not
either practical nor desirable now.
We may, however, confiscate excess profits.
(We will have been helped in these policies by
the rigid war-time control which has already been
established over industry. Intervention by the
government in management is now common. In this
sense the militarists have helped us.)
E. Japan's economy must be placed on a peaceful, non-milita-
ristio basis. (We assume that Japan will lose her colonies.)
1. The armament industries must be liquidated and their
plants converted to peaceful produotion.
2. Industrialization must be increased, must be more
highly developed, and must be more balanced.
After the war we fortunately will have:
) A mueh larger heavy industrial base.
(b) A large body of workers with new mechanical
skills.
(o) An industry capable of produoing mchine
tools, mmchinery, and other precision work.
We should therefore avoid the pre-war over-comcentration
on textiles and other light industry. Our industry
can be more diversified with greater emphasis on
quelity and presisiem work. (Important in this conversiom
will be the ratiomal use of the industrial progress made
by Japan during the wer.)
aacs eesmmplim tais suédealy; but should mke a
 a large mrket for mmehinery and betser
Jepan's agrieultusal prodmetion.

=== Page 294 === (confidence 0.97)
-3 -
A stert should be made toward the liquidation of feudalism
In land holding.
 1. The land of absentee landlords should be purohased by
the government.
2. This land should be mde available to land-poor peasants
on eesy terms. (Okano apparently prefers, however,
that tityle to the land remain with the government.)
3. Part of this land should be used for State demonstration
farms to experiment with and show the farmers the
actual advantages of full mechanization.
4. Through these State farms, and by other means, there
should be propaganda to encourage the peasants to
engage in group or cooperative farming, thus over-
coming the present conditions of exoessive sub-
division and soattered holding. The goal in this
will be voluntary collective farming.
(In this, also, the militarists have helped us
by their present intensive propaganda for
cooperative farming as a war measure.)
G. The living and working conditions of the workers must
be improved.
1. There should be the eight hour working day.
2. Factory l@gislation should fix minimum standards of
working conditions and treatment of workers.
3. Laws should proteot the rights of the workers for:
(a) Colleotive bargaining
(b) Recognition of unions
(o) The right to strike
(d) Social insurance.
(We do not expect to have minimum wage laws
at this stage.)
II. PARTICIPATING GROUPS
The Comunist Party will seek and welcome cooperation with
any roups whioh will support this general program.
This willingness to join with any group will extend to
the capitalists.
Our minimm program is the two main points: anti-militarism
and demooraoy. We will insist on these. We will not
insist on all other features of the program, such as
cooperativeyfarming, if they meet with general opposition.
III. FORM OF GOVERNMENT
There should be set up a "Peoples Democratio Government".
This will be composed of representatives of all demosratio
groups. These will inolude: bourgeoisis, liberals and
Coists.
(The inelusion of the Communists in the Government is of
the utmest impertanee. They have been the galy active
foree Fighting against the war. The liberals In Japan
sen wenk and leeking in courage and determination.

=== Page 295 === (confidence 0.98)
PION OF FIRST STAGE
The length of this period will depend on two factors:
the progressive oharacter of the government itself;
the treatment of Japan by the Unit4 Nations.
1. If the government gives the people reasonable seourity
and happiness, it will be considered successful and will
last a long time. If it does not satisfy the majority
of the people, attempts will be made to overthrow it.
The government must therefore be progressive and re-
sponsive to the democratic desires of the people.
2. Its duration also depends on the attitude of and post-war
settlement imposed by the United Nations. If they
impose impossible indemnities, and make the position of
the eountry or the condition or the people seem hopeless,
they will rise against the government. If they give
Japan a fair settlement, the government will be able
to improve the general condition of the people and the
government will thus be stabilized.
(We can say that change toward more extreme forms of
government -- either progressive or reactionary --
will be slower if the condition of the bulk of the
people is fairly satisfactory. We prefer the slower
development.)
SECOND STAGE (Completion of the Bourgeois Demooratic Revolution)
The first stage cannot immediately accomplish the liquidation
of all feudal remnants in our society, partioularly on
the land.
In the second stage we hope to complete the liquidation of
these feudal traces and set up complete democracy on a
capitalistic basis.
There will be no distinct break between these two stages:
the transition and development will be gradual. The first
will merge into. the second. Some parts of the program
for the first stage may not be accomplished until the
second: part of the second may be aohieved while in most
other respeots we are still in the first stage. By the
end of the second stage, we should have liquidated all
foudal remnants, such as:
The Emperer
The nobility, Privy Council, House of Peers, "mlder
Statesmen".
The special privileges of the military
(The Army and Navy will be completely demoeratized)
The sooial disorimination against the Eta (outcasts)
 T (oili)
The aim of this stage will be to carry the development
through to full socialimm. This will mean the lsquidation

This development in Japan will be more rapid than in Chine.
Feudalistie remnants are not as strong in Japan as in

and in addition, capitalism is more developed in Japen

Japan, however, will be behind the more advanoed Western
